===PAGE 3=== (ocr, 29 lines)
   1|                                                                                    In Memoriam
   2|                                                    Jean Elizabeth Hobbs, a dedicated and energetic member of the Lunenburg community for
   3|                        more than 60 years. She graduated from Northbridge High School in 1944 and went on to study at Memorial Hospital
   4|                        School of Nursing in Worcester, graduating in 1947. That same year she married Frederick B. Hobbs and moved to Lunen-
   5|                        burg, where he would later become Police Chief.
   6|                        She was a contributor to the town history, Lunenburg: The Heritage of Turkey Hills, 1728-1978 and played key roles in
   7|                        planning the town's 250th and 275th anniversary celebrations.
   8|                        In the 1960s, she was a Girl Scout leader, Den Mother, and a founding member of the Lunenburg Music Aiders. She served
   9|                        on the Town's 1976 Bicentennial Committee, the Insurance Advisory Committee, Board of Directors for the Memorial Fund
  10|                        and for School Community Linked Services and was a member of the Lunenburg Historical Society. She was a fixture at
  11|                        Lunenburg's Annual Town Meeting, never reluctant to share her thoughts and opinions.
  12|                        Jean was dedicated to elder services. She was Chairwoman of the Lunenburg Council on Aging from 1990 to 2001. She
  13|                        served on boards for the Central MA Agency on Aging, the Central MA Association of Councils on Aging, and the Monta-
  14|                        chusett Opportunity Council and its Nutrition Advisory Board. She also served as a SHINE (Serving Health Insurance
  15|                        Needs of Elders) counselor at Eagle House. In the early years of Fitchburg Access TV, she hosted Senior Lifestyles. She
  16|                        was a familiar face at Eagle House where she contributed hundreds of volunteer hours and was a member of the book club.
  17|                        Jean was instrumental in securing a state grant to expand Eagle House Senior Center in 1998. In 1998 she received a DOVE
  18|                        Award from the Massachusetts Legislature in recognition of her dedicated volunteer service to elders.
  19|                        For many years Jean was the Lunenburg correspondent for the Fitchburg Sentinel and the Times Free Press. From 2001 to
  20|                        2008 she worked for The Lunenburg Ledger and wrote a popular column called "From the Eye of the Eagle" in which she
  21|                        voiced her views on town politics and happenings, colored with a bit of local history.
  22|                                                Lawrence W. Marshall
  23|                        Larry was born in Leominster and attended Leominster High School and Amherst College. He was active in the Boy Scouts
  24|                        of America since his youth and continued his compassion for service with his youngest son's Troop 1798 of Lunenburg;
  25|                        proudly assisting them to achieve the rank of Eagle Scout. He owned various businesses in the area, and was proud to be a
  26|                        well-respected real estate appraiser for over 40 years. He was first an extremely devoted husband and father and enjoyed
  27|                        spending time with his family and gardening. Looked on as a mentor, advisor and friend to many, Larry will be sorely
  28|                        missed and forever remembered and loved.
  29|                                                                                                      3

===PAGE 6=== (ocr, 37 lines)
   1|                                                                            LUNENBURG PROFILE
   2|                                                                           YEAR INCORPORATED- 1728
   3|                                                                            REGISTERED VOTERS-7115
   4|                                                                                FY' 16 TAX RATE-$19.62
   5|                            OPEN TOWN MEETING GOVERNMENT- FIVE MEMBER BOARD OF SELECTMEN
   6|                                                      ANNUAL TOWN MEETING- 1ST SATURDAY IN MAY
   7|                                                     ANNUAL TOWN ELECTION- 3RD SATURDAY IN MAY
   8|                                                                            AREA- 26.63 SQUARE MILES
   9|                                                                              ROAD MILES- ABOUT 100
  10|                       Lunenburg was settled in 1718 and incorporated in 1728. It occupies a land area of
  11|                       approximately 26.4 square miles. Lunenburg is located in north central Massachusetts
  12|                       approximately 43 miles from Boston. It is bordered on the west by the Town of Ashby and the
  13|                        City of Fitchburg, on the south by the Town of Lancaster and the City of Leominster, on the east
  14|                       by the Town of Shirley, and on the north by the Town of Townsend.
  15|                       Local legislative decisions are made by an open town meeting consisting of all registered voters
  16|                       in the town. Subject to the legislative decisions made by town meeting, the affairs of the town
  17|                       are generally administered by a five member Board of Selectmen and a Town Manager. Local
  18|                       school affairs are administered by an elected seven member School Committee while local taxes
  19|                       are assessed by a three member Board of Assessors, all are appointed on an at-large basis for
  20|                       staggered three-year terms.
  21|                       Lunenburg is a semi-rural community located in northern Worcester County. Three water bodies
  22|                       account for somewhat of a clustered approach to the town's historical development. Lake
  23|                        Whalom and the former Whalom Amusement Park delineated the Whalom District from the
  24|                       remainder of Lunenburg. This district was developed as a housing base for many factory
  25|                        workers working in the mills and factories of Leominster and Fitchburg. In many ways the
  26|                       characteristics of the Whalom District more closely reflect characteristics of these cities than of
  27|                       the rest of Lunenburg. The Hickory Hills area of town, around Hickory Hills Lake, was
  28|                        developed as a private community comprised largely of vacation cottages. The third body of
  29|                       water is Shirley Reservoir, a public water body also developed with mainly vacation cottages
  30|                       that have now been modified to year-round residences. Commercial and industrial development
  31|                       is permitted in several areas of town, primarily along the two major arteries (Routes 2A and 13),
  32|                       and the town's peripheries, due largely to the close proximity of Leominster's and Fitchburg's
  33|                       sewer and water systems. Although many farms and orchards had slowly developed into single
  34|                       family housing, farming continues on in Lunenburg. At the Annual Town Meeting on May 4*h
  35|                       2013, the Town voted to establish an Agricultural Commission to represent, preserve, and
  36|                       enhance farming in the community and to administer the "Right to Farm" bylaw.
  37|                                                                                                      6

   1|                                                                      ADMINISTRATION
   2|                                                                                                     17

===PAGE 18=== (ocr, 38 lines)
   1|                                                                                      REPORT OF THE
   2|                                                                                     TOWN MANAGER
   3|                       The calendar year of 2016 was a year of progress for the Board of Selectmen and Town Manager's
   4|                       Office. During 2016 there are many accomplishments I would like to highlight:
   5|                             • In February the Building Reuse Committee presented the results of the Feasibility Study on the
   6|                                   Building Assessment and Space Needs of the town departments performed by Tappe Associates.
   7|                                   The preferred option of the Committee was to demolish parts of TC Passios School and renovate
   8|                                   portions due to the sufficient space and cost. The Board agreed to move forward on this
   9|                                   alternative with the possibility of renting/leasing a portion of that building. At the Annual Town
  10|                                   Meeting it was voted to transfer the custody and control of the T.C. Passios and Brooks House
  11|                                   from the School Committee to the Board of Selectmen. Further, at the November 2016 Special
  12|                                   Town Meeting the Building Reuse Committee submitted an article to convey for the purpose of
  13|                                  leasing or selling the Ritter Building, Town Hall, old Primary School, Brooks House, and T.C.
  14|                                   Passios. This article did not receive a majority vote but the use of these buildings continues to
  15|                                   be a topic of interest.
  16|                             • During the month of March the Board signed a three year Collective Bargaining Agreement with
  17|                                   the Professional Firefighter's Association and the Board discussed the role and responsibility of
  18|                                   the Selectmen, including being a liaison to various committees. Also in March DPW Director
  19|                                   Jack Rodriquenz presented an updated Pavement Management Plan for roadway improvements
  20|                                   of all town roads that included a proposal for a $4 Million dollar voter authorized debt exclusion
  21|                                   that passed at both a town meeting and ballot vote. The work will be done over a 4 year period
  22|                                   and would be financed over a period at least twice as long. The Town will attempt to put an
  23|                                   additional $50,000 each fiscal year in the Pavement Management Line Item Account, so there
  24|                                   would be sufficient funding for roadway improvements going forward.
  25|                             • The Summer Street redevelopment and roadway improvement project continued to move
  26|                                   forward with a slated beginning of 2017. The total project costs of $1OM will be funded through
  27|                                   the Regional Transportation Improvement Plan (TIP), which are state and federal transportation
  28|                                   funds.
  29|                             • The Town received notice in May that the Tennessee Gas Pipeline Company had suspended any
  30|                                   further work on the Northeast Energy Direct Project, which the Town had declared opposition to
  31|                                  in February of 2015.
  32|                             • The Annual Town Meeting on May 7"' passed a balanced budget for FY 2018 with an omnibus
  33|                                   budget of $33,802,328. Amendments to the FY 2018 budget were made at both the August and
  34|                                   November Special Town Meetings for a revised total of $34,068,192.
  35|                             • The Special Town Meeting on August 22nd                              provided for a surplus of state revenue to be
  36|                                   transferred into the reserve fund and a portion into the school budget. The STM also approved
  37|                                   funding for the DPW Collective Bargaining Agreement and approval of two rooftop solar
  38|                                                                                                     18

===PAGE 19=== (ocr, 36 lines)
   1|                                                                                         REPORT OF THE
   2|                                                                                        TOWN MANAGER
   3|                               projects at the Middle/High School and DPW buildings as well as a power purchase/net
   4|                               metering agreement for a project at a non-town owned parcel. Zoning articles as well as
   5|                               a boundary line agreement on Chestnut Street and a 10% match for an APR property for
   6|                               Lanni Orchards was on the warrant.
   7|                          • The Selectmen heard a presentation from World Tech Engineering on the Complete Streets pro-
   8|                               gram under Mass DoT in October and this would be continued into 2017.
   9|                          • The Special Town Meeting on November 28 included amending the FY 2017 budget, creating a
  10|                               Town Building Stabilization Fund, appropriating money to improve the Cemeteries, updating the
  11|                               Salary Administration Plan, and amending the FY 2017 Cable Access budget.
  12|                         • A Request for Proposals was advertised for Legal Counsel Services with proposals due in De-
  13|                               cember and a final choice has yet to be made as of writing this report.
  14|                         • The Selectmen received the resignation of former Town Manager Kerry Lafleur in July and the
  15|                               Town began the process of searching for an Interim Town Manager and subsequently a perma-
  16|                               nent Town Manager. Interim Town Manager David Marciello was appointed in September and
  17|                               served until December. The search for a permanent Town Manager began in September with a
  18|                               final decision made in December to appoint Heather Lemieux.
  19|                          At the time of writing this report I have worked for the Town of Lunenburg for just under three
  20|                          months. In my short amount of time on the job, it is apparent there is an abundance of wonderful
  21|                         people that make up the Town of Lunenburg. Thank you to the Board of Selectmen for the level of
  22|                         respect that each of you gives your fellow members, the staff, and the public and well thought out
  23|                          decisions. Thank you to my Executive Assistant, Elaine Peterson, for all your hard work and keep-
  24|                         ing the office running smoothly. I would also like to thank the department heads and staff for the
  25|                          high level of service they provide to the residents of town. Specifically, I would like to express grati-
  26|                         tude to the department heads that lead our various departments: James Marino, Police Chief; Patrick
  27|                          Sullivan, Fire Chief; Jack Rodriquenz, DPW Director; Karen Brochu, Town Accountant/Finance Di-
  28|                         rector; Myleen Mallari, Treasurer/Collector; Louise Paquette, Assessors; Steve Malandrinos, IT Di-
  29|                          rector; Adam Burney, Land Use Director; Susan Doherty, COA Director; Martha Moore, Library
  30|                          Director; Kathryn Herrick, Town Clerk; and all their staff for their talent and working together to
  31|                          make our town run as efficiently as possible. Lastly, thank you to the residents and businesses that
  32|                          make Lunenburg a wonderful place to visit, work, and live.
  33|                          Respectfully submitted,
  34|                          Heather R. Lemieux
  35|                          Town Manager

   1|                                                                        TOWN SERVICES

===PAGE 52=== (ocr, 25 lines)
   1|                                                                               BUILDING DEPARTMENT
   2|                            The Building Department is located on the second floor of the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts
   3|                            Avenue, Lunenburg, MA. Office hours for the Building Department are Monday and Wednesday 8:00 A.M.
   4|                           to 4:00 P.M., Tuesday and Thursday 8:00 A.M. until 6:00 P.M., closed Friday. Michael J. Sauvageau serves
   5|                           as the Building Commissioner, John Morreale serves as the Assistant Building Inspector and Lisa Nor-
   6|                            mandin serves as the Administrative Assistant.
   7|                            The Building Department processes all Wiring, Plumbing and Gas permits, this can be done by mail or in
   8|                            person during regular business hours.
   9|                            Building permits for 2016 increased slightly from 452 in 2015 to 500 for 2016. Solar permits remain steady
  10|                           as well as new single family homes and townhouses. The Emerald Place (formerly Whalom Park) project is
  11|                           now completed. Several large projects are in the reviewing stage or have been approved and are anticipated
  12|                            to begin construction soon. The new middle/high school is now complete and absolutely beautiful.
  13|                           The 2009 International Building Code for residential and commercial construction is the current building
  14|                           code and it is anticipated that a new Ninth Edition Code (2015 International Building Code) will soon be in
  15|                           effect.
  16|                            The new energy code will be in place on January 1, 2017 based on 2015 International Energy Conservation
  17|                            Code with the "stretch code" amendments/. Residents and developers are reminded that Lunenburg is a
  18|                            "Stretch Code Community" which has specific code requirements in addition to the current energy
  19|                            code requirements.
  20|                            Residents should be aware that Massachusetts Law requires that all persons, partnerships and corporations
  21|                            who bid or perform residential contracting (reconstruction, alterations, additions etc.) for work exceeding
  22|                           $1000.00 be registered with the Commonwealth. This office will enforce this provision; however you
  23|                            should ask your contractor about his/her registration. Please contact this office with any questions about this
  24|                           requirement or call for any information regarding zoning requirements or building code issues.
  25|                                                                                                     52

===PAGE 54=== (ocr, 44 lines)
   1|                                                                        CONSERVATION COMMISSION
   2|                        The Town of Lunenburg Conservation Commission is a seven member commission appointed to 3 year terms by the
   3|                       Board of Selectmen, whose duties are codified under the Conservation Commission Act MGL c. 40 § 8C. The
   4|                        Commission enforces the Massachusetts Wetland Protection Act MGL c. 131 § 40 and Article XXII of the local
   5|                        bylaws for the protection of wetlands.
   6|                        The year 2016 was, as last year, one of a very busy and active agenda containing both public hearings and meetings
   7|                        for a wide range of projects from commercial development, subdivisions and small single family upkeep projects.
   8|                        The Conservation Commission worked to spell out goals of preparing to use the year 2017 to complete revising its
   9|                        regulations to provide consistency with the original bylaw and to find those sections that may be now out of date and
  10|                        need to be modified. This effort should conclude by the fall of 2017.
  11|                        The Commission held numerous inspections with the Commission Administrator on weekends prior to meetings to
  12|                        familiarize themselves with the upcoming proposals that would appear on their agenda. The Commission is also
  13|                        working with the town on familiarizing itself with the upcoming storm water mandates from the EPA and working
  14|                        towards compliance both by procedure and with any necessary codification.
  15|                       The Conservation Commission in cooperation with the Open Space Committee and the Lunenburg Snow Riders
  16|                       Club has closed out a grant that obtained the former Lane property, known as the Holman Conservation Area, as
  17|                       open space and has worked to place an adequate and ADA-complaint parking area and bridges that would blend in
  18|                        with the trail heads on Holman Street. The Conservation Commission also held public recreational walks through its
  19|                        open space parcels and plans to continue to do so.
  20|                       The Commission has worked closely with the Lake Shirley Improvement Corporation and Hickory Hills Lake
  21|                        Management Group on updates of their management plans and becoming actively involved in formulating the new
  22|                        updates designed to give a better picture of the overall health of the ecosystems and the various ways to ensure
  23|                        continued protection of these valuable resources.
  24|                        The Commission also obtained over $16,000 in grant money from DCR for the completion of forest stewardship
  25|                       planning on the Holman Conservation Area, both Town Forests, the open space on Robbs Hill and the Cowdrey Lot.
  26|                       The Commission retained Gary Gouldrup, a noted forester with many years of experience working in town for the
  27|                        commission and private landowners.
  28|                        This year also marked the hiring of former commission consulting agent Matthew S. Marro as the new Conservation
  29|                        Administrator. With his 18 years of dealing with commission projects and goals, Mr. Marro was ready to hit the
  30|                        ground running on day one. Mr. Marro has worked with the commission this year on office goals and procedures,
  31|                       increased inspections and office productivity as well as special projects. For example, he publishes a monthly article
  32|                       in the Lunenburg Ledger addressing the commission goal of informing the public on local environmental and
  33|                       recreational issues.
  34|                       The Commission thanks all the town agencies for a great year and look forward to many more!
  35|                       Respectfully Submitted,
  36|                       Richard Bursch, Chairman
  37|                       Todd Dwyer, Vice-Chairman
  38|                       Tom Bertram
  39|                       Kenneth Jones
  40|                       Carl Luck
  41|                       Bob Pease
  42|                       Jack Rabbitt
  43|                       Matthew S. Marro, Conservation Administrator

   1|                                                                                 COUNCIL ON AGING
   2|                                                                              July 1, 2015 - June 30, 2016
   3|                       The mission of the Council on Aging is to advocate for the improved quality of life for all seniors through
   4|                       supportive services, programs and education. Councils on Aging were established under Massachusetts
   5|                       General Law Chapter 40, §8B, to include setting policy for the administration of elder programs or
   6|                       services and to develop, coordinate and conduct such activities which advocate for elder issues.
   7|                       The FY16 Council on Aging staff consisted of Susan Doherty, Director; Amanda Koeck, Administrative
   8|                       Assistant and Transportation; Faith A. Anderson, Outreach Coordinator; Joanne Brazell, Meal Site
   9|                       Coordinator; Jeanne Tatro, Ruth Swiecicki, Judy Alario and Elsa Watson, Meal Site Assistants; Jerry
  10|                       Beloin, Jim McGuigan, and Paul Grunditz, MART Van Drivers.
  11|                       The Council on Aging Board is an eleven member board appointed by the Board of Selectmen. The FY16
  12|                       Council on Aging Board members were; Chairperson Pete Lincoln, Vice-Chairperson Jacquelyn Dwyer,
  13|                       Betty DiGiacomo, Sarah Grant, Brian Guenard, Deb Lincoln, Kevin McNally, Cheryl Moisan, Diane
  14|                       Nowd, Jane Rabbitt, and Judith Tarbell.
  15|                       The Town budget alone does not support this senior center. Other sources of funding are the Eagle House
  16|                       Supporters Inc., a non-profit 501(c) 3 group, Formula Grant funding from the Commonwealth of
  17|                       Massachusetts, and Montachusett Regional Transit Authority.
  18|                       We are very lucky to have a large volunteer force at the Eagle House. In FY16 we had 43 volunteers who
  19|                       contributed 3,149.75 hours of their time to help us out by doing things such as driving for the Meals on
  20|                       Wheels Program, program leaders, kitchen helpers, office help, library, and friendly visitors. We greatly
  21|                       appreciate their service.
  22|                       The Council on Aging widened its connection with the community by changing the name of the senior
  23|                       center to "Eagle House Senior Community Center" and now welcomes all people age 50 or older in
  24|                       Lunenburg and surrounding communities to participate in our activities. A new Facebook page was also
  25|                       created by COA board member Jacquelyn Dwyer. The Eagle House extended its hours of operation to
  26|                       include evening programming on Wednesdays.
  27|                       A $3,000 grant was received from the Nashoba Valley Healthcare Fund with financial support from the
  28|                       Greater Lowell Community Foundation and the Community Foundation or North Central Massachusetts.
  29|                       This grant was used to reinstate the "Are You OK?" telephone reassurance program. This program
  30|                       provides free daily computerized wellness calls to homebound elders or people with disabilities. These
  31|                       calls are generated through the Nashoba Valley Regional Dispatch District.
  32|                       The Eagle House offers a variety of programs, such as health and wellness, arts and crafts,
  33|                       intergenerational, bocce, cards and games, cultural, information and referral, and social events both at the
  34|                       senior center and offsite. There were 778 people who utilized the senior center in FY16. Those 778
  35|                       people compiled 21,409 units of service by participating in the programs listed above.
  36|                                                                                                     55

===PAGE 59=== (ocr, 51 lines)
   1|                                                                       DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
   2|                                   The Department of Public Works combines the resources of the Highway, Park, Cemetery, Sewer, and
   3|                        Facilities divisions, and Vehicle Maintenance services into one department organizing and scheduling personnel
   4|                        and equipment to best serve the residents of the community through the effective delivery of an extensive scope
   5|                        of services with a high degree of efficiency.
   6|                        DEPARTMENTAL OBJECTIVES
   7|                                    "Public Works is either working or on call twenty four hours a day three hundred and sixty five days a
   8|                        year to respond to any weather, infrastructure, roadway, or facility related emergency that may arise."
   9|                                    We strive to enhance the awareness of the value of Public Works provides to our Town, and ensure oth-
  10|                        ers are aware of our contribution. This is not for an expectation of praise, but rather to make sure efforts are not
  11|                        forgotten as they merge into the fabric of our everyday service.
  12|                                   Major events over the past years have highlighted the fact that Public Works has a seat at the table and is
  13|                        a major player in emergency management. Town Officials, Police, and Fire acknowledge that Public Works is
  14|                        critical in order to facilitate the required services of others. We must continue to build on this awareness.
  15|                                    This department must also reach out to the public we serve and educate them to the fact that the common
  16|                        services they receive do not just happen, but rather are the efforts of a dedicated staff of individuals, service
  17|                        companies, and vendors. Safe roadways, wastewater control, solid waste collection and recycling operations,
  18|                        tree removal, storm water management facilities and grounds maintenance, winter snow and ice removal, ceme-
  19|                        teries and parks are the expected services supporting everyone in the community.
  20|                                   We will work to engage the public in order to set reasonable expectations and gather support for annual
  21|                        budget requests, capital requests, and specific projects.
  22|                        PAVEMENT MANAGEMENT
  23|                                    The updated Pavement Management Plan, inclusive of an electronic database and a revised Town-wide
  24|                        pavement defect analysis. For a second time, a complete survey of every road was completed to establish a
  25|                        "pavement defect value." The amount of new paving does not keep up with the yearly deterioration factor, and
  26|                        as such the backlog is in excess of nine million dollars. The program remains underfunded however efforts of the
  27|                        Town Manager and the Board of Selectmen have resulted in a separate line within the DPW budget earmarked
  28|                        solely for paving and paving related services.
  29|                                    Road Projects typically extend over two construction seasons and overlap from one fiscal year to the
  30|                        next. During this year the paving schedule included: a large portion of Howard and West St., West Townsend
  31|                        Rd., Northfield and Flat Hill Rd. each funded through the Chapter 90 State Aid program with the exclusion of
  32|                        Flat Hill Rd. completed through the operational budget of the DPW by a dedicated Pavement Management Plan
  33|                        line. Due to a hellacious winter of 2014-2015, the Governor allocated a sum of money to each city/Town in the
  34|                        Commonwealth under the description of "Winter Rapid Recovery Program" to be used exclusively for paving.
  35|                        The completion of Howard St., a portion of Reservoir Rd., Arbor St., Page St., Kilburn St., and West St. all ben-
  36|                        efitted from the program with a curb to curb overlay along the most degraded portions.
  37|                                    Documentation provided through a "pothole" log (a daily record of road work specific to patching and
  38|                        filling of pot holes) provided information that one third of all the available work days during the season are dedi-
  39|                        cated to patching and other temporary repairs to similar roadway failures. As defects are identified we respond to
  40|                        every location in a timely manner. This has proven to be as less than effective method due to the fact that patch-
  41|                        ing is merely a temporary fix. New pot holes appear each spring particularly after a severe winter, and often staff
  42|                        addresses the same areas year after year and must continue to do so until full curb to curb rehabilitation is com-
  43|                        pleted, clearly a revenue driven operation.
  44|                                    The seasonal crack sealing program has been effective in some locations however this application is de-
  45|                        signed as a temporary solution as well with an effective life span of around five years. Many streets that have
  46|                        been "sealed" are well beyond that time frame. The mechanic of the process involves the installation of rubber-
  47|                        ized asphalt under high pressure into cracks or separations in the pavement. The Pavement Management Plan
  48|                        identifies specific areas where this method is the best option based upon the aforementioned pavement defect
  49|                        value This application does not take the place of curb to curb paving, only postpones it and is not applicable in
  50|                        areas of more serious defects.
  51|                                                                                                     59

===PAGE 60=== (ocr, 48 lines)
   1|                                       The revised Pavement plan, no matter how detailed, is of little value without an identified source of
   2|                            funding. Lack of revenue continues to be the major detriment to improving the overall condition of our road-
   3|                           ways. The primary sources of funding are the Massachusetts Department of Transportation Chapter 90 pro-
   4|                           gram and the dedicated line in the DPW budget. The estimated value of our 86 miles of paved streets is, by
   5|                           industry standards, calculated to be 62 million dollars. The General Highway maintenance line within the
   6|                            operational budget has remained virtually unchanged for two decades while the overall omnibus budget has
   7|                           nearly tripled to a value in excess of 30 million dollars. In order to initiate and sustain a road improvement
   8|                            program a substantial infusion of funds is necessary. The conservative estimate remains at approximately
   9|                           1.2 million dollars per year total or an additional $800,000.00 to existing allocations. Applying $400,000-
  10|                           $500,000 a year to a backlog of work that approaches 10 million dollars dooms the current plan to failure.
  11|                            As an example: many roads are compromised to the degree that only a major reconstruction can bring them
  12|                           to an acceptable quality and less travelled, local roads remain out of reach.
  13|                           FLEET MAINTENANCE
  14|                                       The DPW is responsible for the repairs and preventative maintenance to all Town vehicles and
  15|                            equipment. High performance police units, First responder Fire and Rescue apparatus, park, cemetery, and
  16|                            school vehicles as well as over 30 pieces of highway related machinery are serviced by department person-
  17|                           nel. Outsourcing of highly technical repairs has become more frequent and therefore more costly. Due to the
  18|                           volatile global oil market, the overall cost of fuel and petroleum based products continues to be a moving
  19|                           target and therefore difficult to estimate annually.
  20|                                       The installation of a vehicle maintenance software program has proven to be a valuable tool. Each
  21|                           repair is logged by department and vehicle identification number and memorialized in a data bank. Every
  22|                           vehicle can be tracked for part numbers, required services, as well as frequency and cost of repairs over the
  23|                           life of the equipment. As Director I have created and submitted to Town Officials a vehicle replacement
  24|                           schedule created by the American Public Works Association in order to more clearly represent our needs.
  25|                           FACILITIES MANAGEMENT
  26|                           The following is a list of buildings and properties maintained by the DPW:
  27|                                            DPW Garage 520 Chase Road                                     Public Safety Complex
  28|                                            Park Bldg. Leominster Rd.                                     Town Hall
  29|                                            Cemetery Building, Holman St.                                 Eagle House
  30|                                             Town Beach                                                   Town Common
  31|                                            Ritter Bldg.                                                  Teen Center
  32|                                            Old Primary School                                            Veterans Park
  33|                                            All athletic fields/playgrounds excluding school properties
  34|                                 The ever increasing workload made necessary the addition of a second individual to assist with repairs
  35|                           and maintenance. The increase in staff by only one has significantly lessened the need for outsourcing, spe-
  36|                           cifically preventative maintenance. Staff remains on-call 24 hours a day 365 days per year. Staff keeps
  37|                           buildings compliant and protects the safe running of the work environment.
  38|                                  Any significant interruption of service within Town buildings carries the potential of disrupting or disa-
  39|                            bling necessary services provided to the community. The facilities support staff are problem solvers and
  40|                           technicians with a wide range of experience encompassing all of the construction trades. They deliver ser-
  41|                            vices or oversee those functions provided by specialized subcontractors. Staff manages and forecasts all of
  42|                           the building assets and prioritizes replacement costs. Unfortunately this component of the program has fallen
  43|                           short of expectations and need and with the formation of a Building Reuse Committee it is clear that asset
  44|                            management relative to our buildings requires a professionally prepared capital plan. With the use of build-
  45|                           ing maintenance software, requests for service are acknowledged immediately, prioritized, and completed in
  46|                           a timely manner. Those individuals requesting service are contacted upon completion of the tasks a history
  47|                           of the repair is filed electronically. Although a success, this remains a reactionary approach.
  48|                                                                                                     60

===PAGE 61=== (ocr, 50 lines)
   1|                         ENVIRONMENTAL
   2|                         Storm Water
   3|                                    For yet another year the Environmental Protection Agency has not set the requirements for the next
   4|                          phase of storm water regulation and permits. As we await the potential of what will be an unfunded Federal
   5|                         mandate the Town remains in compliance with current protocol.
   6|                                    The DPW, as a result of limited funding is forced to become reactionary to roadway drainage issues. A
   7|                         capital program in the 1990's earmarked $20,000.00 annually toward rehabilitation/installation of piping and
   8|                         catch basins. The elimination of this dedicated funding placed the financial burden upon the overall General
   9|                          Highway maintenance allocation effectively reducing drainage repairs. In anticipation of the new regulations
  10|                         the Town Manager has re-established a budget line within the DPW dedicated to storm water, a necessary first
  11|                          step.
  12|                                     As roadways are scheduled for pavement, drainage is surveyed and necessary repairs are addressed to
  13|                          avoid paving over problems. New storm water regulations will likely force the Town to become more proac-
  14|                          tive albeit with no identified source of funding. At the conclusion of winter and again in the late fall drainage
  15|                          structures in close proximity to open water are cleaned Public Works attempts to maintain an aggressive
  16|                         schedule of street sweeping and catch basin cleaning, the front line of storm water management.
  17|                         Waste Water
  18|                                     The DPW continues as the caretaker of the entire sewer system. To become more efficient the Sewer
  19|                          Commission has outsourced much of the day to day inspections and preventative maintenance of the overall
  20|                          wastewater system inclusive of 10 pump stations. Business management, inspections, permitting, and over-
  21|                          sight of the subcontractor remain the responsibility of the DPW.
  22|                          Tree Removal
  23|                                     While it may seem difficult to believe, the infamous "Ice Storm of '08" continues to detrimentally af-
  24|                          fect trees in the Town right-of-way. As presented to the Board of Selectman during the initial recovery pro-
  25|                         cess, the "heartwood" of a large volume of trees was affected. Previously unnoticed damage together with nat-
  26|                          ural attrition of every species creates a backlog of tree felling and pruning that far exceeds the allotted budget.
  27|                          The department is unable to fulfill each resident request and must prioritize work based upon the calculated
  28|                          degree of danger to residents and property. With the establishment of a Hazardous Tree Survey, a more effi-
  29|                         cient approach to defects has been established by prioritization. Sadly, the amount of work far exceeds the
  30|                          available revenue. Until a plan is in place, the DPW will act in a reactionary mode. All work aside from brush
  31|                         removal, chipping, and the felling of small diameter trees is outsourced due to the necessity of a bucket truck
  32|                         and a licensed professional. Each spring DPW staff initiates the activity of removing and disposing of woody
  33|                         debris within the right-of-way and a Town-wide program of curbside mowing. A hugely aggressive program
  34|                          by our power provider has lessened the burden of the Town to remove those trees that may cause an interrup-
  35|                         tion of electrical service to the community.
  36|                         Landfill
  37|                                    The "capped" landfill off Young's Rd. remains in compliance with the inspection protocol mandated
  38|                         by the Division of Environmental Protection. The site is monitored regularly for excessive methane gas as well
  39|                          as the possibility of groundwater contamination.
  40|                                    The frequency of the testing was reduced through a special permit acquired as a result of consistently
  41|                         favorable results. The landfill site including the perimeter fencing is inspected on a regular schedule by DPW
  42|                         personnel.
  43|                                     Adjacent to the "cap" is a storage area for catch basin materials and street sweepings both determined
  44|                          by the EPA to be hazardous waste. We are permitted to store these materials through the procurement of a
  45|                         (BUD) Beneficial Use Determination permit. The DEP requires yearly testing of these materials. Other com-
  46|                          munities are required to transport these tainted materials to an appropriate site at an excessive cost.
  47|                                     On weekends during the fall and springtime months DPW personnel oversee and manage the annual
  48|                         composting of brush, leaves and other organic debris providing a valuable service to our residents and add a
  49|                         valuable component to our recycling program. There has to date been no dedicated funding toward
  50|                                                                                                     61

===PAGE 62=== (ocr, 49 lines)
   1|                                    the organization or long range remediation of this location. Once again, dollars will be taken from
   2|                        the General Highway line to maintain this location while keeping it environmentally safe. It is likely this de-
   3|                        partment will present a plan to use the compost as leverage to clean and grade the area as well as the removal
   4|                        of the brush. With water and sewer at the perimeter this sandy, gravelly area adjacent has been proposed as a
   5|                        potential recreational are for the Town. As of yet this project has not gained traction.
   6|                        Solid Waste
   7|                                    Public works monitors, orders, stores, and distributes trash bags in order to facilitate the curbside
   8|                        collection of trash and recyclables for our by our current vendor, Casella Inc for the pay as you throw pro-
   9|                        gram.
  10|                        Lake Shirley Dam
  11|                                    In the early 1990's, with the structure in need of repair/rebuilding the Lake Shirley Association
  12|                        turned over ownership of the dam to the Town. After completion of a total reconstruction, responsibilities for
  13|                        maintenance, monitoring, and record keeping was completed solely by a small number of volunteers from
  14|                        the Lake Association. In 2004-2005 custodianship of the dam, compliance with State dam regulations, the
  15|                        establishment of an operations manual as well as an emergency action plan was placed under the umbrella of
  16|                        the Department of Public Works. As with the storm water program and other EPA / DEP mandates compli-
  17|                        ance with current and future regulations is critical. We rely on the expertise and support of a professional
  18|                         consultant to assist with predetermined mandates. The most recent inspection resulted in a positive report.
  19|                        Winter Operations
  20|                                    Snow and ice present a major challenge to our Town. The safety of the general public depends on
  21|                         the ability of the DPW to maintain our roadways in a safe condition despite the severity or the duration of
  22|                         snow and ice events. To that end we have developed a winter operations procedure based upon a detailed
  23|                        plan that must be flexible due to the unpredictability of each storm. A consistently high level of service is
  24|                         expected and must be delivered to every part of Town in a timely manner.
  25|                                    Departmental preparedness begins with successful implementation of the capital plan. Financial
  26|                        support is necessary to provide effective fleet rotation insuring minimal interruption of services due to break-
  27|                         downs and repairs. In the fall, salt and sand are delivered to the Chase Rd. facility. Lunenburg is in a consor-
  28|                         tium with 14 other communities in an effort to procure the lowest possible price for road salt an absolute ne-
  29|                         cessity. Individually the Town also solicits bids for road sand. From late summer to early fall vehicle mainte-
  30|                        nance staff inspects and prepares the trucks, sanders and plows for winter operations. During the construc-
  31|                         tion season road crews address right-of-way issues, overhanging branches, and pavement defects in order to
  32|                        eliminate obstacles to plowing. Each division within the DPW is involved in winter operations. Typically
  33|                         DPW staff combines with 18-20 subcontractors to provide service.
  34|                                    Generally it is not the amount of snow but the duration of the event that determines the overall cost.
  35|                         For example, a fast moving snow storm of 6-8 inches will cost significantly less than a long duration freez-
  36|                        ing rain event even though the accumulation is much less. Historically, the department operates in a deficit
  37|                        due to a yearly allocation that is below the cost of an average winter. It is a dedicated group of individuals
  38|                        who successfully challenge the worst Mother Nature has to offer regardless of severity or duration.
  39|                                                                        "DPW workers are the original Storm Troopers"
  40|                        PARKS
  41|                                    In conjunction with the three elected Park Commissioners DPW staff oversees and maintains all
  42|                        parks and grounds exclusive of school property. A full time Park employee was replaced with seasonal
  43|                        workers. Much of the Town wide landscape maintenance is outsourced, resulting in significant savings and
  44|                        increased efficiency. Repair of the multi-use track complex is in the planning stage. After many years, a mu-
  45|                         tual effort between the Commissioners and all users of the facility is gaining traction.
  46|                         CEMETERY
  47|                                    One Cemetery Division Superintendent, a DPW employee, handles the duties of the North and South
  48|                                   cemeteries, maintaining the grounds, coordinating burials, and meeting regularly with the three
  49|                                                                                                     62

===PAGE 63=== (ocr, 9 lines)
   1|                                     members of the Cemetery Commission. The DPW supplies equipment and support staff for, on aver-
   2|                           age, forty burials a year and general maintenance of the facilities.
   3|                                      As always, I am deeply appreciative of the continued patience and support of our residents and fellow
   4|                           Town employees as we persevere.
   5|                                     "As caretakers of the Town's infrastructure our goal is to preserve and enhance the resources of the
   6|                           Town of Lunenburg for future generations. To that end we strive to meet or exceed all expectations associat-
   7|                          ed with the challenges of each of the divisions under the umbrella of public works."
   8|                                                                                           Jack Rodriquenz
   9|                                                                                                 Director

===PAGE 64=== (ocr, 42 lines)
   1|                                                                                 FINANCE COMMITTEE
   2|                                   (This is a reprint of the message printed in the May 7, 2016 Annual Town Meeting warrant)
   3|                           The Town Budget to be presented at Town Meeting has followed the same process as in recent years. The
   4|                           Town Manager has again provided Department Heads a "target budget' to work from, based on the financial
   5|                           forecast, current service levels and funding opportunities.
   6|                           The Finance Committee is recommending approval of the Town budget in the amount of $33,802,328.00,
   7|                           with an increase in revenues from all sources, totaling $1,758,413 over the 2016 Budget (after recap). A sig-
   8|                           nificant increase is anticipated in State Aid from Chapter 70 (education funding) and general government aid
   9|                           (lottery). While this is welcome news, unrestricted general government aid has yet to reach its previous
  10|                           2006 levels. Furthermore, since the financial forecast was first presented, health insurance costs have come
  11|                           in much greater than expected. For the most part, Town departments have continued to maintain a consistent
  12|                           portion of the budget, but pressures from a few drivers, namely Retirement, Health Insurance and Debt Ser-
  13|                           vice, are beginning to demand a larger share, creating constraints on growth.
  14|                           In breaking down the revenue to the Town, 67% comes from real estate property taxes and 33% comes from
  15|                           other sources such as State Aid, motor vehicle excise or meal taxes, betterments and other fees. The effects
  16|                           of such aid on particular aspects of the budget are difficult to parse out, except for Chapter 70 funding, which
  17|                           accounts for $1 of every $3 budgeted for the schools.
  18|                           Retirement and health insurance consume an ever increasing portion of the total budget. Town employees
  19|                           pay into 1 of 2 retirement systems. Licensed school district employees contribute to the Massachusetts
  20|                           Teacher Retirement System, which requires no additional town funding. Any additional funding of this Re-
  21|                           tirement System is done at the State level, and is reported in notes to the Town Financial Statements as an
  22|                           'On Behalf' payment. Non-licensed school personnel and Town-side employees contribute to the Worcester
  23|                           County Retirement System. The Town makes two payments to this system, an employer contribution for
  24|                           active employees and an assessment to fund the 'Unfunded Liability' of future payments to retirees. The
  25|                           Retirement Assessment has gone up 8.7%, or $84,143, over the 2016 Budget (after recap). Health insurance
  26|                           represents a 9.1% increase in spending and a 0.3% increase in budget share. The Town has been able to ne-
  27|                           gotiate changes to health plans over the past few years to help reduce cost, moving to a 75% Town, 25% em-
  28|                           ployee cost sharing. This year premiums are projected to increase by 12.8%, which affects both the Town
  29|                           budget, and the School Department budget.
  30|                           Even if the funding was available to do so, the Finance Committee and Town Administration have expressed
  31|                           reluctance to add new positions to departments until the issue of future liability to the Town for both retire-
  32|                           ment and other post-employment health benefits (OPEB) are adequately addressed. In the meantime, where
  33|                           it is possible to consolidate current positions to better meet the needs of the Town, such changes have been
  34|                           proposed. For example, consolidating a part time administrative position and a contracted consultant into
  35|                           one full-time position in the Conservation Commission office and adding hours to existing positions in Infor-
  36|                           mation Technology department and in the Assessors' office to meet increased workloads.
  37|                           Debt Service has 14% budget share, up a quarter percent with a 6.6%, or $298,911 increase, the main reason
  38|                           being the new Middle/High School, as well as existing debt from the Primary School, Public Safety Build-
  39|                           ing, and Library. We are still several years out from seeing these projects paid down. The Capital Planning
  40|                           Committee has recommended funding 12 items at $780,975; this budget funds 10 items at $619,475. There
  41|                           is no request or recommendation to borrow money to fund the remaining items.
  42|                                                                                                     64

===PAGE 65=== (ocr, 23 lines)
   1|                           There is however a warrant article to see if the Town is willing to fund the Pavement Management Plan
   2|                          through a debt exclusion. This will not impact the budget in FY17 but if approved would increase the debt
   3|                          service starting in FY18. The Finance Committee will make its recommendation on this article at Town
   4|                          Meeting.
   5|                          Because Retirement, Health Insurance and Debt Service are taking up more of the budget, general spending
   6|                          for the School Department, Public Safety, and the DPW are seeing decreases in budget share.
   7|                          The Police and Fire Chiefs continue to express a need for additional staffing to meet an ever more demand-
   8|                          ing work load, but the requests have not been accommodated in this budget. Regional Dispatch will be add-
   9|                          ing another community which will reduce Lunenburg's assessment and free up $7,000 to increase weekend
  10|                          coverage at the Public Safety Building.
  11|                          The School Committee budget request is only $21,072 greater than the amount in the budget presented here,
  12|                          which assumes the Governor's recommended budget for Chapter 70 funding makes it through the final ver-
  13|                          sion of the Commonwealth's budget. The additional Chapter 70 funding reduced the target gap by $291,523.
  14|                          In summary, the budget presented addresses at least current service levels with little room to discuss priori-
  15|                          ties beyond nondiscretionary items. The Finance Committee will continue to assure the budget addresses the
  16|                          priorities of the Town of Lunenburg as we understand them. We congratulate the Town administration's
  17|                          work to maintain the AA+ bond rating which allows the Town to borrow at competitive rates, saving taxpay-
  18|                          ers significant funds over time. We thank all of the town employees for their continued service, and are
  19|                          grateful for the continued cooperation of the Town Manager, Finance Director, and each department head to
  20|                          ensure that every question is answered.
  21|                                                                                    Mark Erickson, Chair
  22|                                                       Terri Burchfield, Vice Chair                           John Henshaw, Secretary
  23|                                                         Karin Menard, Member                                     Jay Simeone, Member

   1|                                                                         INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY
   2|                           The primary mission of the Information Technology Department is to increase efficiency through the use of
   3|                          technology. The IT Department enables all other town departments and schools to communicate and collab-
   4|                           orate, manage daily tasks, organize, store, and share information, and generally conduct Town business
   5|                          more effectively. We also provide the necessary infrastructure, equipment, software, and expertise to com-
   6|                          ply with current regulations and methodology when interacting with outside agencies and organizations.
   7|                          The Information Technology staff for the Town has remained constant this year, but we have added a new
   8|                          full time IT position for the Schools. Following is an overview of our current IT staff:
   9|                           Steve Malandrinos - Information Technology Director
  10|                          • Hired in December, 2012
  11|                           Previously an engineer at Cisco Systems, IT Director for the Town of Belchertown, MA and Belchertown
  12|                           Public Schools
  13|                           Daniel Nadareski - Network Administrator
  14|                          • Retired police officer from the Albany, NY police force with over 24 years on the job
  15|                           Prior experience includes computer crime and forensics, network administration, video security system ad-
  16|                           ministration, and IP phone system administration
  17|                          Jordan Pochini - Tech Support Specialist
  18|                          • Recently graduated from Fitchburg State University with a degree in Computer Science
  19|                           Prior experience includes both individual contributor and management roles in retail environments
  20|                           Nicholas Scoggins - School Technology Coordinator
  21|                           • Received Associate and Bachelor of Science degrees from ITT Technical Institute
  22|                           Served 5 years as Security System Administrator at Greater Lawrence Technical School
  23|                           Along with serving as helpdesk for all users' daily technology issues and the usual maintenance and sched-
  24|                           uled replacement of the town's and schools' technology assets, the IT Department has been involved with
  25|                          several high impact projects throughout the past fiscal year. The following examples are among the more
  26|                          noteworthy improvements that IT has implemented.
  27|                           An entirely new backup and disaster recovery solution was implemented at Town Hall, the DPW, and the
  28|                          Ritter Memorial Building. Backups are a low visibility, high impact area of IT. They're never needed until
  29|                          they are, and then they are absolutely critical to the stability of the organization.
  30|                           A large portion of the Technology department's time this past year was dedicated to the transition from the
  31|                           previous Lunenburg High School to the new Lunenburg Middle-High School. Much of our critical infra-
  32|                           structure was centralized at the old building, requiring detailed planning and work over many nights and
  33|                           weekends to maintain the services on which our school faculty and staff depend to do their jobs. We also
  34|                           spent much of the summer building, configuring, and bringing online the many new systems that are cur-
  35|                           rently serving our students, faculty and staff.
  36|                           We continue to strive to leverage limited resources to improve efficiency wherever possible. I am very
  37|                           happy to continue in this role, and I could not be more pleased with the level of skill, hard work and dedica-
  38|                           tion of my staff, Daniel and Jordan. I know that because of them we will continue to improve the technolo-
  39|                           gy landscape in Lunenburg while maintaining the positive reputation we have strived for over the past sev-
  40|                           eral years.
  41|                                                     Steve Malandrinos, IT Director

   1|                                                                           LUNENBURG PUBLIC LIBRARY
   2|                            The library has had a really good year! In fact I think the Lunenburg library is just AWESOME!!! During
   3|                            the year we welcomed Heather Buiwit as our new Digital Services Librarian. Heather handles all things E (e
   4|                            -books, -e-reference tools, e-audio, e-book training, etc.) as well as assisting me with the library's technolo-
   5|                            gy (Wi-Fi, trouble shooting computer issues, documenting technical practices). Heather is also helping out
   6|                            with marketing the library. She recently created a new Facebook page for us and is the moderator and con-
   7|                           tent provider for the page. Jocelyn Lacroix, our new Library Page arrived last fall and has acclimated quite
   8|                            well.
   9|                            The Board of Trustees has remained the same this past year although changes are due with this next election
  10|                            cycle. This year the Trustees have focused heavily on updating our library policies and a few building pro-
  11|                           jects. I wish to thank them personally for all their support and guidance.
  12|                            The library building has had a relatively quiet year. Our new burner has given us wonderful even AND con-
  13|                            sistent heat throughout the building. The new building management system has enabled the DPW to get
  14|                            ahead of any critical events - as it notifies them when it something isn't working properly. This year we had
  15|                            the two quiet study rooms and the story craft room painted and they look great. Our thanks to the Friends
  16|                            organization for funding the painting projects. The Green Community Task Force accepted our grant pro-
  17|                            posal for the migration of interior lights for the library and the project should be completed by the end of
  18|                            March 2017. We are hoping this gives us better and less costly lighting. The library invested in new tables
  19|                            for the Community Hall and the Story Craft Room. We also added additional security cameras.
  20|                            The library has increased the number of programs we provide including a series of exhibits of local artists'
  21|                            work and a monthly series called Friday Food & Flicks where we provide the movie and attendees bring
  22|                            their favorite recipes based on a specific theme like soup, casseroles, appetizers and desserts. We have add-
  23|                            ed a new and enlarged continuous book sale so people can come and browse a large selection of adult fiction
  24|                            and non-fiction to purchase with the proceeds going to the Friends group. We will of course continue with
  25|                            the Friends much loved June & October book & bake sales.
  26|                            The library has been focused heavily on improving our collections over the last year. In the main library we
  27|                            have added (and will continue to add) e-books that can only be borrowed by holders of a Lunenburg library
  28|                            card so you can borrow that bestseller a bit quicker than before. We now subscribe to digital versions of all
  29|                            the local newspapers that residents can access online (see our website). We began a very large project of
  30|                            overhauling the entire nonfiction collection. Hopefully this will be completed by this summer.
  31|                            The library is becoming the community center it was meant to be. We are partnering with many local organ-
  32|                            izations and providing a venue for them to connect with Lunenburg residents including Community Health
  33|                            Connections, Lunenburg Historical Society, Boy Scouts, GiftMart, Freedom's Way, Eagle House/Senior
  34|                            Center and many more.
  35|                            FROM THE CHILDREN'S ROOM
  36|                            The Children's Room is just bursting with all types of activities. We have reached out and are partnering
  37|                            with three different schools to provide story times for children once a week. The Children's Room now hosts
  38|                           a series called STEM Saturdays; the program has activities to nurture an interest in science, technology engi-
  39|                            neering, and math. Our LEGO programs continue to challenge boys and girls alike. The Children's Room
  40|                            began selling "book bundles" - 3 books on the same theme (each bundle is $1) and it has become a great hit.
  41|                            The money goes to the Friends organization which enables us to buy special things like a new dollhouse and
  42|                            a new CD player.
  43|                                                                                                     68

===PAGE 69=== (ocr, 34 lines)
   1|                          The Children's Room has continued its project of renewing and expanding the collection. We have added a
   2|                          new JJ (birth to 8) non-fiction collection. This collection of material supports children's quest and enthusiasm
   3|                          for learning by providing a viable, up-to-date collection of books and media. These items will inform chil-
   4|                          dren about current issues (i.e. climate changes and global warming, best practices for supporting a healthy
   5|                          planet, current events, etc.) as well as history, science, art. A new Chapter Book area has been created to
   6|                          highlight this material and make it easier to locate for those burgeoning readers
   7|                           Two new computers were added to the Children's Room to accommodate the increased number of children
   8|                          coming in. We now provide access to ABCMouse. org, an amazing website that creates a unique, fun learning
   9|                          environment that the children LOVE! We have added a homework survival kit and an online version of
  10|                          World Book.
  11|                           Our future programming must encompass a broad spectrum of interests and ages. Our goal is to continue age/
  12|                          developmentally appropriate programs for preschool age children, as well as develop programming for school
  13|                          age children and families. The Children's Room will continue to provide quality, well-vetted resources and
  14|                           games using today's technology as well as the time-tested models such as a parent, a cozy chair, and a great
  15|                          book!
  16|                          FROM THE TEEN ROOM:
  17|                          In 2015 we began examining the young adult collection, focusing on the manga section. We continued this
  18|                          effort in 2016, focusing on the graphic novels, non-fiction, and classics sections. Each section was reviewed
  19|                          to make sure that titles were in good shape, contain accurate information, and have strong teen appeal. In an
  20|                          effort to make browsing easier, the biographies were separated out of the non-fiction section and made into
  21|                          their own collection. A freestanding spinning rack was also introduced. The spinner houses the newly creat-
  22|                          ed romance, inspirational fiction, and teen CD collections.
  23|                           One of our primary goals is to provide access to materials in as many varied formats as possible. To that ef-
  24|                          fect, much to the delight of the teens, anime DVDs were introduced. While this is still a fledgling collection,
  25|                          it fills a much remarked up gap in the collection and has been met with a very positive response.
  26|                          The gaming center was expanded to include not just video games but board games as well. The board game
  27|                          collection targets all ages not just teens. It houses two player games, family friendly games, and party games
  28|                          in a mix of classic and contemporary styles. A monthly teen board game night was created to introduce teens
  29|                          to new games and allow fellow gamers a chance to connect.
  30|                           We will continue to update the teen room, introducing new materials, programs, and technology as we strive
  31|                          to make the teen room the best, most responsive space it can be.

   8|                                                            provider.

  10|                                                             acquire, record, broadcast and re-broadcast programming from both town
  11|                                                            functions and meetings, as well as programming from publicly available
  12|                                                            sources.

  17|                                                            concerts and local events, just to name a few. The remaining 16% of
  18|                                                             programming was obtained through other media sources and online
  19|                                                             downloads.

   1|                       The strategy for the PAC studio in the upcoming Fiscal years includes acquiring new studio space
   2|                       to provide a better environment for producing programming. Part of the plan is to purchase more
   3|                       cameras and equipment for loan to increase public participation in creating content. We would
   4|                       like to increase visibility to the public to encourage more loaning of equipment, studio usage and
   5|                       submitting of videos from the public.

   7|                       room for live meeting broadcasts, received a much needed upgrade of equipment in FY16. A
   8|                       new HD video server/playback system, CASTUS, was installed in the Spring of 2016. This new
   9|                       server allows for easier recording of meetings, remote LIVE broadcasts, and remote bulletin and
  10|                       scheduling capabilities. This upgrade allowed PAC to provide higher quality broadcasts and help
  11|                       to prepare the station for HD broadcasts when Comcast makes it available.
  12|                       This equipment upgrade accounted for 31.69% of the FY16 expenses.
