   1|                                                         SENIOR PROPERTY TAX WORK-OFF PROGRAM
   2|                                                                                              Est. 1998
   3|                                                  CALENDAR YEAR JANUARY 1, 2016 - NOVEMBER 1, 2016
   4|                          The Property Tax Work-Off Committee is a 5 member committee appointed by the Board of Selectmen.
   5|                          The Lunenburg Property Tax Work-Off Program offers income eligible senior citizens age 60+ an oppor-
   6|                          tunity to receive up to $1,000 off of their property tax bill in the calendar year rather than the fiscal year.
   7|                          Participants worked in various town departments to earn the tax abatement doing general office duties, cus-
   8|                          todial, library shelving, and kitchen assistance.
   9|                          For each hour worked a person received $10 and worked up to 100 hours between January 1, 2016 and No-
  10|                          vember 1, 2016 to receive the abatement. Any hours worked beyond that were considered volunteer hours.
  11|                          There were eleven Lunenburg residents who participated between January 1, 2016 and November 1, 2016.
  12|                          These individuals, as a whole, accumulated 837.25 hours towards their abatements.
  13|                          Eligibility Requirements:
  14|                          • Applicants must be 60 years of age or older
  15|                          • Own and occupy a home in Lunenburg as their primary residence for at least one year
  16|                          • Receive a property tax bill in their name or their spouse's name who is also 60 years of age or older
  17|                                Meet the income guidelines of : One person household - $40,000 / Two person household - $60,000
  18|                          • All new applicants are required to have a CORI background check and must re- apply and be accepted
  19|                               annually for participation in the program.
  20|                          Earnings are exempt from state taxes only. Earnings are subject to Federal and FICA tax withholdings and
  21|                          are also subject to OBRA (Omnibus Budget Reconciliation Act of 1990, otherwise known as MA Deferred
  22|                          Compensation), if participants are not receiving a pension from either Worcester Regional Retirement or
  23|                          Massachusetts State Teachers Retirement.
  24|                          Departments who participated between January 1 - November 1, 2016:
  25|                          Board of Selectmen
  26|                          Facilities and Grounds
  27|                          Board of Health
  28|                          Building Department

===PAGE 76=== (ocr, 48 lines)
   1|                                                                                 SEWER COMMISSION
   2|                        The five member Sewer Commission was originally established at the Special Town Meeting on May 6, 2006.
   3|                         At the first Sewer Commission meeting after the May 2015 Town election (5/26/15) when the Commission
   4|                        elects the officers for the new fiscal year, Carl Luck was elected to remain as Chairman, Mike Nault to serve
   5|                        as Vice-Chair and Troy Daniels to serve as Clerk. Sue Bunish was elected to the Commission by ballot in the
   6|                         May 2015 election, however neither Dave MacDonald nor Butch Bilotta sought re-election, leaving one vacan-
   7|                        cy on the 5-member Commission. On July 7, 2015 John Reynolds was appointed to the Commission to serve a
   8|                        one-year term by a joint meeting of the Board of Selectmen and the Sewer Commission. The Sewer Commis-
   9|                        sion bears the responsibility of overseeing the Town's sewer system, planning for the future, and developing
  10|                        and implementing rules, regulations and policies. The Department of Public Works manages and maintains the
  11|                        operational side of the system. Under the Town's Charter, responsibility for the day-to day operation, care and
  12|                        maintenance of the sanitary sewers are under the supervision of the Town Manager. The Town of Lunenburg
  13|                        does not operate a sewer treatment plant. Approximately 83% of Lunenburg sewer is discharged to the City of
  14|                        Leominster and the other 17% is discharged to the City of Fitchburg.
  15|                        There are currently 846 properties connected to the Lunenburg system and another 393 that have access to
  16|                         Town Sewer but are not yet connected. During FY16, 37 new accounts connected to Town Sewer. The Com-
  17|                        mission billed out $734,145 in quarterly sewer bills, and had $63,086.97 of prior year delinquencies added to
  18|                        the owners' property tax bills for non-payment. Three properties with access to the original sewer installation
  19|                        were assessed privilege fees that had not originally been assessed betterments. Privilege fees help to pay down
  20|                        the debt owed by the Town on the original sewer project.
  21|                        At the May 2015 Town meeting the Commission had gone before the townspeople to request an additional ap-
  22|                        propriation of $300,000 for the addition of Pleasant View Ave into the SRF project. It was approved and in
  23|                        June 2015, the engineers at Wright Pierce began the engineering and design to install sewers in the Pleasant
  24|                        View Ave. in concert with the SRF project, providing great cost saving by having the same construction firm,
  25|                        CJP & Sons Construction, complete the installation while their equipment was still in town. On July 15, 2015
  26|                        construction to install sewers for the SRF Sewer Project began in the Pratt Street and Lakeview Avenue areas.
  27|                        Work on the sewer installation continued throughout FY16. In the fall of 2015, Wright Pierce again assisted the
  28|                        Town by helping the Town obtain a DEP grant of $30,000 for the inspection and evaluation of over 300 man-
  29|                        holes to determine causes of Inflow/Infiltration (I/I). Wright Pierce began the project in February 2016 and
  30|                        completed the inspections and analysis in the spring 2016. The Commission will use their evaluation and rec-
  31|                        ommendations to alleviate Inflow/Infiltration going into FY17.
  32|                        In February 2016, the Commission approved revisions of the Sewer Extension Regulation, thanks in large part
  33|                        to the hard work of Troy Daniels. The Commission was grateful to Troy in his work to help protect the Town
  34|                        by putting forward a complete and comprehensive Regulation. The Commission continued to work hard on the
  35|                        Grease Trap Regulation to make it a workable document that can be easily followed by all Food Service Estab-
  36|                        lishments (FSE) that have internal or external grease interceptors. The Commission held two public hearings to
  37|                        get input for those restaurants and well as the townspeople. As of May 2016, the regulation was approved, and
  38|                        a new fee structure and requirements for annual permits, compliance and reporting was instituted. Lunenburg
  39|                        has 18 food service establishments with grease traps and interceptors. This regulation will continue to be
  40|                        strongly enforced as Fats, Oils and Grease (FOG) can negatively affect the smooth operation and maintenance
  41|                        costs of the sewer system. While the Grease Trap Regulation focuses on commercial and public disposal, indi-
  42|                        vidual home owners can also contribute a significant amount of grease into the system which increases every-
  43|                        one's costs. Whether on sewer or septic, grease should not be put down the drain.
  44|                        In August 2013, the Sewer Commission began contracting with Small Water Systems Services of Littleton
  45|                        (SWSS) for the Operations and Management of the 10 pumping stations and sewer infrastructure. The Sewer
  46|                        Commission has been very satisfied with the personnel, the work and the arrangement with SWSS, renewing
  47|                        the contract for two successive years.
  48|                                                                                                     76

===PAGE 77=== (ocr, 37 lines)
   1|                            In April 2016, anticipating the end of the three-year renewable contract, the Sewer Commission put the
   2|                            maintenance of the sewer system out to bid. Small Water was awarded the bid and we are happy to contin-
   3|                           ue this great partnership for 3 more years. The new contract, effective 7/1/16, was expanded to include the
   4|                           generators in each pump station and quarterly meter readings for manual and irrigation accounts.
   5|                           At the May 2016 Town Meeting, the Commission put forward 5 articles, 3 of which related to the budget
   6|                           and stabilization funds. For the standard budget article to appropriate funds for FY17, $69,775.27 was trans-
   7|                           ferred from Retained Earnings to cover both general expenses and debt service on the betterments. The goal
   8|                           of the Sewer Commission is to maintain a balanced budget going forward and to build up the Retained
   9|                           Earnings so that it may be used for major capital projects to maintain and improve the operating efficiency
  10|                           of the system. The fourth article updated the Sewer Bylaw to mandate the holding of 2 public hearings to
  11|                           ensure public participation in regulation changes. The fifth article was to present the updated Comprehen-
  12|                           sive Wastewater Management Plan (CWMP) for acceptance. The CWMP needed updating since the origi-
  13|                           nal master plan was put into place in 2010. Wright Pierce had completed the original evaluation and plan
  14|                           and worked with the Commission to update the CWMP. This master plan is a great source to the Town for
  15|                           checking compliance and future planning.

  21|                           Richard St. Cyr was appointed in October 2016 and we thank them all for their willingness to serve on the

  23|                            grateful for his knowledge and counsel. Jay Simeone, a past member of the Sewer Commission, serves as

  27|                            Facilities) who whenever called upon, provides his expertise and service to the Sewer Division. They
  28|                           would also like to acknowledge and thank the Lunenburg Water District for their partnership and good
  29|                           working relationship with the DPW and especially with Business Manager, Barb Lefebvre, in providing
  30|                           valuable assistance with meter readings, ownership changes, and status updates.

  17|                               Provide information on tax abatements

   4|                           -345-4146, extension 430.
   5|                           The Board of Health, with the assistance of its agent, the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health, continued
   6|                           its mission to maintain my maintain its focus on public health and safety issues during 2016, reviewing Title
   7|                           V Inspection Reports, building permits, septic and well permits, nuisance investigations, housing inspec-
   8|                           tions, complaints and general public health related issues.
   9|                           This past year has seen the reinstatement of the Septic System Repair Program which helps low to moderate
  10|                           income families replace failed septic systems or connect to public sewer. Anyone interested in finding out
  11|                           more about this program can contact the Board of Health office.
  12|                           Homeland Security planning is ongoing. Meetings continue between our agents, other town Departments
  13|                           and regional entities to develop a plan for not only Lunenburg but with our partner towns in our region.
  14|                           This year, we processed applications for Septic Permit Lot Tests, permits for new construction, new wells,
  15|                           repairs or upgrades to septic systems and well permits. The Board continues its vigilance with regard to sep-
  16|                           tic systems, wells, food service inspections, housing and nuisance complaints and its general mandate to pro-
  17|                           tect the environment and groundwater of the Commonwealth.
  18|                           The Board of Health provides numerous other services in cooperation with our Health Agents, the Nashoba
  19|                           Associated Boards of Health. In addition to our environmental services, numerous clinics are provided
  20|                           throughout the year to our residents. These clinics provide inoculations, blood pressure screenings, Well
  21|                           Oldster Clinics and dental screening. fluoride treatment and dental checks of our youngsters in the school
  22|                           system are provided by the Nashoba dental staff.

  24|                           monitors the mosquito population in Lunenburg and advises of any potential diseases such as EEE and West
  25|                           Nile Virus and sprays frequently to control the population during the warmer times of the year.. Residents
  26|                           who want to have their property sprayed can do so by emailing to: cmmcp@cmmcp.org, calling (508) 393-

  28|                           office.

  30|                           illness and Hospice care and counseling to those in need of this service.

   1|                                                                           TOWN OF LUNENBURG, MASSACHUSETTS
   2|                                                                                               FIRE DEPARTMENT
   3|                              CHIEF                                                                                                                    655 Massachusetts Ave
   4|                          Patrick A. Sullivan                                                                                                          Lunenburg, MA 01462
   5|                          TEL: (978) 582-4155
   6|                          FAX: (978) 582-4033
   7|                                                                                                      INCO
   8|                                             The Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians of the Lunenburg Fire Department are
   9|                                          dedicated to providing the highest quality, most cost effective fire and emergency medical services to
  10|                                                                               the citizens of the Town of Lunenburg.
  11|                                                  In 2016 your Fire Department responded to 1986 calls for service, broken down as follows:
  12|                                                  Fires & Hazardous Conditions                    475                Emergency Medical 965
  13|                                                  Inspections                                     508                Other                       38
  14|                                                  Fire Department staffing today is five Career and forty-forty-five On Call Firefighters and
  15|                                        Emergency Medical Technicians. The station is manned seven days per week, but only from 6:00 a.m. -
  16|                                        6:00 p.m. During those hours the station is manned with two-three Firefighter/Emergency Medical
  17|                                        Technicians, plus the Chief of Department and EMS Coordinator weekdays. This coverage is a
  18|                                        combination of full-time personnel in conjunction with 72 hours per week of per diem coverage. The per
  19|                                        diem coverage is provided by our Call Firefighter/EMT's manning the station at their hourly call rate.
  20|                                                  At night however, the station is unmanned and all of our personnel respond from home on an "on
  21|                                        call" basis. This adds several minutes or more to our response time as personnel must respond to the
  22|                                        station, pick up the appropriate equipment and then respond the call.
  23|                                                  It should be noted that except for the addition of forty-eight hours of per diem coverage on
  24|                                        Saturday and Sunday (2 per diem Firefighter/EMTs from 6:00 a.m. - 6:00 p.m. each day), our staffing
  25|                                        model has remained unchanged for over fifteen years. In this same time period we have seen an almost
  26|                                        48% increase in the call volume that we respond to.
  27|                                                  Our call volume in 2016 matched what we experienced in 2015, which was our busiest year to
  28|                                        date. As our community continues to grow I can only anticipate the demand for emergency services to
  29|                                        grow along with it. Once again I must state the need for 24 hour staffing in the station. Funding to
  30|                                        maintain a minimum of two Firefighter/EMTs or Paramedics in the station 24 hours a day, 7 days a week
  31|                                        is critical to maintain the high level of service our townspeople expect. This would allow either the first
  32|                                        ambulance or first piece of fire apparatus to respond immediately. Not only would this reduce our
  33|                                        response times to critical emergencies, it would also reduce the "wear and tear" on our Call members.
  34|                                         This coverage would in no way reduce the need for our On Call personnel; it would only supplement their
  35|                                        response and reduce response times.
  36|                                                  Recruitment and Retention of our Call Firefighters continues to be one of the greatest challenges
  37|                                        for the department and is my main reason to call for more permanent staffing in the station. Make no
  38|                                        mistake, our Call personnel do an outstanding job, and their commitment never ceases to amaze me.
  39|                                        However, like many Call Departments we suffer from a continual turnover in personnel. Public Safety is
  40|                                        a field where training and experience are critical. A new Call Firefighter/EMT must attend over 340
  41|                                        hours of initial training, plus additional time in orientation and field training. Members qualifying to
  42|                                        drive fire apparatus must complete an additional 65 hours of classroom and practical training. On
  43|                                        average, it takes a new member one and a half to two years to properly complete these requirements.
  44|                                         Unfortunately, for a variety of reasons we often do not retain members for more than 5 - 6 years.
  45|                                                                                                     85

===PAGE 86=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|                                    As an example, for the period of 2006-2016 we had a total of 52 Call Firefighters and EMTs
   2|                        leave the department. In that same time we have retained 24 members, or barely half that number. In this
   3|                        same time period, only one of the 5 Career Staff left, and that was a retirement.                                    This trend is most
   4|                        obvious when you look at the years of service within the organization. At the end of 2016, 47% of our
   5|                        department has served for 5 years or less, 63% for 10 years or less. Only 29% of the department has 20+
   6|                        years of service. It should be noted that all of the full-time staff have 20 years or more combined call and
   7|                        career time with the department.
   8|                                    Several of our personnel are members of Regional Technical Rescue units, providing services for
   9|                        Specialized Rescue, as well as Underwater Search and Recovery. They stand ready to respond as needed
  10|                        throughout Northern Worcester County.
  11|                                    2016 was another active year for the Fire Prevention Office, with 508 Inspections, 13 Fire Drills
  12|                        and 16 Public Education initiatives performed. By law we are required to inspect smoke and carbon
  13|                        monoxide detector installations when a home is sold, as well as all new oil burner and propane gas
  14|                        installations. Any business holding a liquor license is also required to be inspected for fire safety issues
  15|                        as well. In addition, all new construction projects must have their plans reviewed for proper fire
  16|                        protection, as well as ongoing inspections during the construction process. Personnel also continue to
  17|                        provide fire safety education to not only children in our schools but adults as well. Other Fire Safety
  18|                        Initiatives undertaken during 2016 include:
  19|                                    - Continuation of a Department of Fire Services Grant in the amount of $6953 to assist with
  20|                                           Fire Safety Education in both the schools and with senior citizens. This grant provides
  21|                                           funding for both materials and personnel to bring fire safety education to our most vulnerable
  22|                                          populations. Highlights of activity through this grant include:
  23|                                                • Educational programs in basic fire safety in Grades K-3, including use of the
  24|                                                      Regional Fire Safety Trailer.
  25|                                                 • Educational programs in conjunction with the Council on Aging. These included
  26|                                                      cooking and heating safety, a CPR Demonstration and Ice safety.
  27|                                    - Participation in the Senior Center's "Sand for Seniors" Program. This program provides
  28|                                          buckets of walkway sand for senior citizens who were unable to obtain it for themselves in
  29|                                          the winter months. In partnership with this program, our Fire Prevention Officer Captain
  30|                                          James Ricci delivers the sand to the residents and when possible, inspects their homes for
  31|                                          smoke and carbon monoxide detectors. If the resident was lacking detectors, Capt. Ricci is
  32|                                          able to install free detectors obtained through the Senior SAFE Grant.
  33|                                    - It should be noted that each year, 50% of fatal fires are found to have nonfunctional or
  34|                                          nonexistent smoke detectors.
  35|                                    Several members are also Certified First Aid and CPR Instructors and provide training to both
  36|                        citizens and town employees.
  37|                                    Our department continues to actively pursue grant funding opportunities as they occur, though
  38|                        those opportunities are becoming scarcer.
  39|                                    Our department service truck was replaced in late 2016. This one ton utility truck with plow is a
  40|                        multi-purpose vehicle used for snow removal, trailer towing and moving of equipment to and from
  41|                        emergency scenes.
  42|                                    Apparatus replacement continues to be a concern. Two of our front line units (Engine 4 and
  43|                        Ladder 1) are 21 and 29 years old years old respectively and showing their age through increasing
  44|                        maintenance costs and "out of service" time. The new Engine 1 is handing a higher volume of responses
  45|                        to decrease the wear and tear on Engine 4, hopefully prolonging its lifespan.
  46|                                                                                                     86

===PAGE 87=== (ocr, 45 lines)
   1|                                       Both units have been submitted for replacement on the FY'18 Capital Plan (Engine 4 having
   2|                           been turned down in FY'17) and it is hoped that due consideration will be given for their timely
   3|                           replacement. The high reliability required of front line emergency apparatus becomes increasingly
   4|                           expensive and difficult to maintain as these units reach the end of their life span.
   5|                                      Firefighter Frank Byrne graduated from the Massachusetts Firefighting Academy's
   6|                           Call/Volunteer Training Program. This intensive four month program provides the basic skills
   7|                           firefighters need to operate in today's challenging environments. All newly hired firefighters are required
   8|                           to complete this program within one year of hire.
   9|                                       In conclusion, I would like to thank the members of the Lunenburg Fire Department for their
  10|                           outstanding commitment to the Town. Their enthusiasm, professionalism and dedication continue to
  11|                           impress me every day. I would also like to thank the other department heads, boards and commissions for
  12|                           their continuing help. Most people do not realize the level of interdepartmental cooperation that occurs in
  13|                           the daily course of town business. My thanks also go out to the citizens of Lunenburg for their ongoing
  14|                           support of public safety. It is that support that allows us to continue providing the services you expect
  15|                           from us.

   1|                                                                                Citizen's Police Academy
   2|                       I would also like to thank and commend the Citizens of Lunenburg who took part in the first annual
   3|                        Citizen's Police Academy. The program was a complete sensation that continues to garner an enormous
   4|                       amount of positive feedback. I am very grateful for the support of the Citizen students who participated
   5|                       and for the generous and unselfish contributions, on the part of the Fitchburg District Court Judiciary, the
   6|                       Deputy Chief of the Fitchburg Police Department, and the Lunenburg Police Officers who volunteered
   7|                       their time, made this program a success. A special thanks to Lieutenant Mike Luth for organizing and
   8|                       facilitating this worthwhile endeavor.
   9|                                                                                                Grants
  10|                       The Police Department participated in the E.O.P.S. Underage Alcohol Enforcement Grant, the Highway
  11|                       Safety Divisions, Traffic Enforcement Grant, MIIA risk management grant, the Office of Justice
  12|                       Assistance Bullet Proof Vest Partnership Program, and the Department of Justice, Community Oriented
  13|                       Police Services Cops Hiring Program and the Commonwealth Security Trust Fund grant Program.
  14|                       The Underage Alcohol Enforcement Grant application was prepared by Sergeant Jack Hebert.
  15|                       Enforcement efforts related to the grant included compliance checks, Party Patrol, and shoulder taps. The
  16|                       program has been extremely successful in the past couple of years with bringing awareness to the license
  17|                       holders in our community as well at risk youth. The Traffic enforcement grant, which has been received
  18|                       by the police department for several years, is now prepared by Officer Tocci, of the traffic enforcement
  19|                       division. The activities include, enforcement for alcohol impaired drivers, aggressive driving and seat belt
  20|                       violations. The program is best known as the Click-it or Ticket program. Lieutenant Luth oversees the
  21|                       facilitation of these programs which have consistently met with a great deal of success.
  22|                                                                            State of the Police Department
  23|                       The Police Department is in operation 24 hours a day, 7 days a week. The desk is open Monday through
  24|                       Friday from 7:00 AM to 7:00 PM. Emergency, Non-emergency and 911 calls are routed through
  25|                       Nashoba Valley Regional Dispatch District, (NVRDD). For all emergencies, dial 911. For general calls
  26|                       please call (978) 582-4531.
  27|                       The public is reminded to report any animal-related complaints to the Regional Call Center at (978) 582-
  28|                       4531. For emergencies, dial 911. More information can be found on the Animal Control website at
  29|                       lunenburgma.gov.

===PAGE 91=== (ocr, 45 lines)
   1|                         Dogs must be licensed by April 1st annually. Dogs must have a current Rabies certificate to qualify for
   2|                        licensing. This can be done at the Town Clerk's office in Town Hall, 17 Main Street Lunenburg, MA
   3|                        01462. Unspayed Female/Unaltered Male: $20 fee. Spayed Female/Altered Male: $5 fee. Late Fee: $25.
   4|                        Patrol Officers were dispatched to 16,682 calls for service this year, investigated 300 motor vehicle
   5|                        accidents, investigated 1405 incidents, conducted 4034 motor vehicle stops, wrote 2714 motor vehicle
   6|                        violations and arrested 490 people.
   7|                         K-9 Jerry completed 25 weeks of continuous training in January of 2015 and over 75 maintenance
   8|                        training sessions to follow. Since that time the K-9 unit has been called out 90 times, deployed 80 times,
   9|                        and conducted 50 tracks. The K-9 unit has conducted 26 narcotics searches, which resulted in the seizure
  10|                        of 1.039 kilograms of narcotics and 2 handguns, and 4 evidence related searches recovering 2 handguns
  11|                        along with other suspect/victim property.
  12|                        The Desk officer answered approximately 7000 calls, fielded some 3400 lobby inquiries, wrote 94
  13|                        incident reports, and 6 field interview reports. This year the day shift desk watch officer was responsible
  14|                        for conducting suicide watch and assisting with the court processing of 112 detainees; either taken into
  15|                        custody overnight or during the day shift. The day shift desk officer processed 131 Firearms Licenses, and
  16|                        registered 13 Sex Offenders. The 3:00 PM to 7:00 PM desk watch officers assisted in the processing of 41
  17|                         detainees while providing suicide watch and numerous other services.
  18|                        Calls for service have increased 42.8%. The arrest rate spiked last year with 636. Although that figure
  19|                        dropped to 490 this year, over the past five years the increase in arrests is up 66.1%. In the past five years
  20|                        reportable incidents, those that require an investigation, and/or court complaint, have increased by 66%,
  21|                        and motor vehicle accidents are up by 21.4%.
  22|                        Overall crime is down slightly with the exception of Group A, crimes against society. In that category
  23|                        weapons violations have increased. In Group B crimes; drunkenness and check fraud have increased.
  24|                         Group A crimes against persons; aggravated assault and sex crimes have also increased. Shoplifting and
  25|                        burglaries have decreased dramatically this year however; larceny, credit card fraud, stolen property, and
  26|                        vandalism have increased in Group A, crimes against property.
  27|                        Growth plays a major role in all of this data. Historically; calls for service, arrests, incidents and accidents
  28|                        continue to climb. Traffic continues to increase, which too, has a direct effect on all of these categories.
  29|                        The demands for service will continue to rise. As I stated in last year's town report, the police department
  30|                        has been asking for an increase in staff since 1996. To prove that point I provided clips from every town
  31|                        report since that time. I've studied other towns of similar demographics, population, commercial entities,
  32|                        bordering communities and found that calls for service, arrests, and incidents were remarkably higher in
  33|                        Lunenburg, yet those communities employed from five to eight more full-time police officers than
  34|                        Lunenburg. National Law Enforcement standards call for 2 1/2 men per thousand population. That would
  35|                        bring the police department up to 22 full time officers. We presently have 14 including the Police Chief.
  36|                        The service the public expects has been steadily declining due to inadequate staffing issues over a long
  37|                        period of time. Again, the time to properly investigate crimes, solve problems by engaging the
  38|                        community, or providing a quick emergency response is continually at odds with the demands of growth.
  39|                         The community will eventually see the Police Department as a useless ineffective service that is a drain
  40|                        on their income and their support for the police will decline. This year I am going to request that new
  41|                        growth fund an increase in staffing over the next several years on an incremental basis. I respectfully ask
  42|                        for the public's support for this initiative.
  43|                         Lunenburg is still a safe place. Despite fiscal challenges, the men and women of the Police Department
  44|                        remain focused and faithful to their mission. I am proud to say, without hesitation, that every Lunenburg
  45|                                                                                                     91

===PAGE 92=== (ocr, 10 lines)
   1|                        police officer works extremely hard to make Lunenburg safe and secure and provide the best service they
   2|                         can to all citizens who live in town, who visit or who are just passing through.
   3|                         Thank you again for another year. I am very proud to serve as your Police Chief. I value the support the
   4|                        citizens of Lunenburg have shown the Police Department. I humbly ask for your commitment as I
   5|                        continue seek funding for additional police officers so I can provide a level of service that you deserve
   6|                        and are entitled to.
   7|                        Respectfully Submitted,
   8|                        Chief James P. Marino

   1|                                                                                 CAPITAL PLANNING
   2|                       Lunenburg's Charter specifically mandates that a Capital Planning Committee prepare a Fiscal Year (FY)
   3|                       Plan and a Capital Improvement Plan. These plans are to be presented to the Town Manager.
   4|                       As noted in the past reports, the criterion for any capital request includes Mandates, Safety, Need, Crisis,
   5|                       Cost Effectiveness and Ability to Fund. The Committee has to review each request under these six
   6|                       elements. Not only do they have to determine that the request fits within the criteria, but they have to
   7|                       prioritize the level that meets the criteria. Therefore, not all requests are included in the FY Plans, mostly
   8|                       due to funding. The result of the proposed FY Plans provides the needed service for safety and protection
   9|                       of the public, the upkeep to our schools and the management of government.
  10|                       At the Annual Town Meeting in May 2016, the following items, totaling $ 619,475, were presented in the
  11|                       FY17 Plan.
  12|                                                         Fiscal Year 2017 Capital Plan

   1|                                                                                    PLANNING BOARD
   2|                       Fourteen ANR plans (plans not requiring approval under the Subdivision Control Law) were approved.
   3|                       Approved residential subdivisions were in various stages of completion. The following continue to build:
   4|                       a nine-lot subdivision on Butterfly Lane (formerly 50 Elmwood Road), Emerald Place at Lake Whalom,
   5|                       Stone Farm Estates off Massachusetts Avenue, and Sequoia Drive off Howard Street. TriTown Landing
   6|                       off Youngs Road completed construction on Building 4 in January 2016 and all 32 units were occupied
   7|                       by May 2016.
   8|                       The Board approved Site Plan Approval applications for Hannaford-To-Go, Safelite Auto Glass, and an
   9|                       auto detailing business; all located on Massachusetts Avenue. Approval was also given for a restaurant at
  10|                       1 Main Street.
  11|                       There was an increase in the number of scenic road public hearings with the designation of two new
  12|                       scenic roads; Northfield Road and Flat Hill Road. After a road has been designated as a scenic road, any
  13|                       repair, maintenance, reconstruction, or paving work done that involves the cutting or removal of trees
  14|                       within the Town way or the tearing down or destruction of stone walls requires Board approval.
  15|                        Lancaster Avenue and Leominster Road are also designated as scenic roads.
  16|                       The appearance of Electric Avenue is undergoing change with the construction of a self-storage facility
  17|                       and Unitil's construction to relocate its distribution and operation center from Fitchburg to the site of the
  18|                       old Whalom Drive-In Theater.
  19|                       2016 also saw the Planning Board revise Section 8.4. of the Protective Bylaw to a more formal Site Plan
  20|                       Approval bylaw which outlines a collection of objective standards to bring consistency to the review
  21|                       process. Additionally, the Board adopted Rules & Regulations for filing and submissions under Section
  22|                       The Citizen Planner Training Collaborative, in conjunction with the Massachusetts Department of
  23|                       Housing and Community Development, and the Urban Harbors Institute, University of Massachusetts
  24|                       Boston, conducts workshops throughout the year. Workshops attended by Board members included
  25|                       Green Communities Program, USDA Rural Programs, Roles & Responsibilities of Planning & Zoning
  26|                       Boards (both Part 1 and 2), Introduction to Subdivision Control Law and ANR, and Planning with
  27|                       Community Support. Some of those workshops are designated (MIIA) which entitles Lunenburg to
  28|                       credit under the MIIA Rewards Program towards public officials' liability insurance.
  29|                       Board members served as representatives to the following committees: Matthew Allison on the newly
  30|                       created Stormwater Task Force, Capital Planning Committee, and continued liaison to the Agricultural
  31|                       Commission; Damon McQuaid on the Building Reuse Committee; Gregory Bittner on the School
  32|                       Building Committee and Green Community Task Force; David Prokowiew as the Montachusett Regional
  33|                       Planning Commission representative and Open Space Committee member; and Kenneth Chenis as the
  34|                       Montachusett Joint Transportation Committee representative.
  35|                       The Planning Office is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts Avenue and can be
  36|                       reached at 978-582-4147, extension 5. Various forms are available online at http://lunenburgma.gov/ on
  37|                       the Planning Board's homepage. The Zoning Map, Protective Bylaws for the Town, Rules and
  38|                       Regulations Governing the Subdivision of Land, and Rules & Regulations for Site Plan Approval &

   1|                                                                           ZONING BOARD OF APPEALS
   2|                          The Zoning Board of Appeals heard sixteen petitions during calendar year 2016. There were several appli-
   3|                          cants seeking special permits and special permits for dimensional variations. The Zoning Board meetings are
   4|                          held on the second and fourth Wednesdays of the month on an "as needed" basis.
   5|                          The Zoning Board consists of the following members: Raymond Beal, Chairman, Alfred Gravelle, Vice
   6|                          Chairman, James Besarkarski, Hans Wentrup and Associate members, David Blatt, Paul Doherty and Sheila
   7|                          Lumi. Lisa Normandin serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Zoning Board.
   8|                          The functions of your Zoning Board of Appeals as required by statures are as follows:
   9|                          To hear and decide applications for Variances from Town's Protective By-Laws as authorized in the By-laws.
  10|                          To hear and decide applications for Special Permits as authorized in the Town's Protective By-laws.
  11|                          To hear and decide appeals in accordance with Chapter 40A, Section 8 of the Massachusetts General Laws, as
  12|                          amended, from any person aggrieved by reason of his or her inability to obtain a permit or enforcement action
  13|                          from the Administrative Officer or Board in the Town, the Regional Planning Agency, abutting City or Town
  14|                          or other Administrative Official.
  15|                          To act upon application for comprehensive permits in accordance with Massachusetts General Laws, Chapter
  16|                          40B. The Zoning Board of Appeals reviews requests for comprehensive permits submitted to the Board under
  17|                          Chapter 40B by developers who wish to create affordable housing. These requests can, by law, circumvent
  18|                          local zoning ordinances.
  19|                          The office of the Zoning Board of Appeals is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts Av-
  20|                          enue, Lunenburg. Business hours are 8:00 AM to 4:00 PM Monday and Wednesday and 8:00 AM to 6:00
  21|                          PM Tuesday and Thursday, closed Friday.
  22|                          If you need any assistance from the Zoning Board of Appeals please contact us at 978-582-4146 X 421 during
  23|                          regular business hours.                Zoning Board applications are available in the office and online at
  24|                          www.Lunenburgonline.gov.

   1|                                                                         LUNENBURG PUBLIC SCHOOLS
   2|                           Our Vision: The Lunenburg Public Schools prides itself on excellence. We provide a child-centered envi-
   3|                          ronment that challenges all learners to achieve their highest potential. This is accomplished through a part-
   4|                           nership with a supportive community.
   5|                           Our Mission: We prepare our students for life-long learning and responsible community membership.
   6|                           Our Guiding Beliefs:
   7|                                      Education is a community partnership.
   8|                                      Learning is reflective, creative, ongoing, and takes place in all environments.
   9|                                      A successful school actively engages students and staff.
  10|                                      All students have talents and can excel.
  11|                                      Professional growth is fundamental to instructional excellence.
  12|                                      All students are capable of learning and are entitled to instructional excellence.
  13|                                      Staff and students are entitled to a safe, healthy and positive learning environment.
  14|                                      An atmosphere of mutual respect is essential for learning.

   1|                                                                                Superintendent's Message
   2|                           What a remarkable year! The District returned to a four school configuration with the opening of the
   3|                       new building and several necessary and attractive upgrades to existing buildings. The completion of the
   4|                       school building project full new opportunity and possibilities elevated the excitement across the schools
   5|                       and community. Our excitement and pride was surpassed only by our feeling of gratitude.
   6|                       Our deepest gratitude is for the voters of Lunenburg who approved the co-located middle-high school
   7|                       project and are paying it forward. This is a school for the entire community and it is our earnest hope
   8|                       that the lives of all our citizens are enriched by this magnificent facility. We are also grateful to the
   9|                       citizens of the Commonwealth and the Massachusetts School Building Authority for its partnership and
  10|                       support of the construction.                We gathered together on October 22, 2016 for the Dedication and Ribbon
  11|                       Cutting Ceremony. During the summer months over 1000 community members had toured the building.
  12|                       Over 200 joined us for the dedication including Congresswoman Niki Tsongas, U.S. House of
  13|                       Representatives, State Representative Jennifer Benson, along with members of the Board of Selectmen,
  14|                       School Building Committee, and the School Committee. Students Julia Wilson, Robert Nash and
  15|                       Brendan Tobin spoke and did a wonderful job representing the students of our schools. We are very
  16|                       proud of them. They are our hope for the future and represent the best in us.
  17|                       The long awaited building was delivered on-time and under-budget. It is everything we hoped for and our
  18|                       dream come true. What a pleasant duty it was to work with everyone associated with this project. A
  19|                       journey that began a decade ago resulted in a splendid modern educational facility due to the enduring
  20|                       dedication, hard work, vision, perseverance, and good judgement of so many. The path was not always
  21|                       straight and contained its fair share of ups and downs and is the result of cooperation, teamwork, and
  22|                                 These are the qualities we continue to embrace as we turn the page and begin a new chapter.
  23|                       A community's schools hold its citizens' hopes and dreams. It is where that which we cherish most, our
  24|                       children, learn and grow. Like our homes, schools are places where our children are nurtured and
  25|                       supported; where children learn to trust and to be trustworthy. Our schools must ever be places where
  26|                       kindness and compassion are in ample supply, freely given and where ideas matter and learning is
  27|                       meaningful. The new building represents a commitment that for generations to come the children of the
  28|                       Lunenburg community will always find a place where hope abides and where dreams are made.

===PAGE 102=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|                       The Jane Doyle Community Playground dedication was held on Wednesday, November 23, 2016. This
   2|                       new playground is located by the Turkey Hill Elementary School Gymnasium. The playground was made
   3|                       possible by the generous donations and support from the Connor Lourens Memorial Fund; the Arnold
   4|                       Family and BEIM Foundation; Turkey Hill Family Lions Club; Lunenburg PTO; the Extended Day
   5|                       Program; and many Lunenburg parents and community members. In addition to the playground,
   6|                       basketball courts with areas for the very popular game "four-square" were constructed. We appreciate
   7|                       everyone who made these wonderful, age-appropriate play areas possible.
   8|                       The Lunenburg School Committee contracted with New England Education Consultants (NEEC) to
   9|                       facilitate a strategic visioning and planning initiative this year. The initial step in this work is a creating a
  10|                       Strategic Framework consisting of a Mission, Core Values, and Vision, all of which serve to set direction
  11|                       for the rest of the planning process. The Strategic Framework is developed members of a Steering
  12|                       Committee working with internal and external stakeholders to help assure the values of the community
  13|                       are well represented and that a connection is made to the public regarding underlying principles for the
  14|                       planning process. The new framework and plan will be adopted by the School Committee by the end of
  15|                       the school year with implementation to begin in the fall. With the opening of the new Lunenburg
  16|                       Middle - High School, the Lunenburg School Committee also committed to develop and implement an
  17|                       adult education program for the benefit of the Lunenburg community. An advisory group was appointed
  18|                       by the School Committee to research and make recommendations by the end of the current school year.
  19|                       Brendan Ryan Tobin was the 2016 Superintendent's Award recipient. Brendan is the son of Thomas and
  20|                       Carolyn Tobin. During his four years at LHS Brendan excelled in honors and advanced placement
  21|                       coursework. He has been the President of the Class of 2017 since 9th grade. Brendan is a poised,
  22|                       humble, and well-spoken young man who represented his school and classmates extremely well. He was
  23|                       honored at the Worcester County Superintendent's Roundtable Scholars' ceremony in January. He
  24|                       selected the following quote from Colin Powell for that ceremony's program. "Success is the result of
  25|                       perfection, hard work, learning from failure, loyalty, and persistence."
  26|                       This past year our schools bid farewell to retirees who devoted many years to public service. Retiring this
  27|                       past year were: Steve Archambault, Middle School Music Teacher; Rhonda Malatos, High School
  28|                       Physical Education Teacher; Judy Haley, Middle School Cafeteria Manager; Linda Papadopoulos, Middle
  29|                       School Special Education Teacher; and Natalie Davulis, Middle School Grade 7 Teacher. When added
  30|                       together these employees have 116 years of service to our students, schools, and community. I hope the
  31|                       benefit our children derived from their dedication, compassion, and hard work is repaid in kind with long,
  32|                       happy and healthy retirements sprinkled with memories of fulfilled careers. On behalf of our school
  33|                       community, I extend to each of them our heartfelt thanks and sincere appreciation for their service.
  34|                       In closing I want to acknowledge and extend special thanks to the PTO, our parents/guardians and the
  35|                       community members who support the PTO. This year the PTO donated $32,000 to the schools to support
  36|                       a variety of opportunities like field trips and educational speakers that are not provided within the school
  37|                       budget. The PTO has a tradition of active engagement and service. The students, faculty, and staff of
  38|                       our schools share this tradition of service to others. It is vital to our mission and our students' future
  39|                       success in life. We are very grateful to the entire Lunenburg community for their support of our students
  40|                       and schools.
  41|                       Respectfully Submitted,
  42|                       Loxi Jo Calmes, Superintendent of Schools

===PAGE 103=== (ocr, 37 lines)
   1|                                                                                         School Facilities
   2|                          Construction of the new Middle-High School (Phase 1) concluded by late May 2016. Shawmut Design and
   3|                          Construction executed the building project with NV5 providing owner's project management services. The
   4|                           School Building Committee continues to provide construction and financial oversight. Phase 2 began shortly
   5|                          after the school year ended with removing all furniture from the former high school, relocating all teacher
   6|                          supplies and equipment to the new school, removal of asbestos in the former high school, then demolition,
   7|                          construction of the new greenhouse and two athletic fields, re-working the student parking lot, tennis courts,
   8|                          construction of an outdoor basketball court and remaining site work. Work was nearly complete by late De-
   9|                          cember 2016. A minor amount of work remains to be completed in the spring of 2017 such as the finish sur-
  10|                          face of the tennis courts, basketball court and installation of an athletic field scoreboard.
  11|                          In February 2016, the Town of Lunenburg released a Request for Proposals for rooftop solar systems to be
  12|                          installed on the new Middle-High School and Department of Public Works garage. The solar array was to be
  13|                          a 20 year agreement that rented roof space and allowed the school district to purchase the electric power at a
  14|                          discounted rate. Solect Energy was awarded the contract to install a 349 kilowatt system that would generate
  15|                          394,000 K WH per year and would be purchased at a fixed rate of 8.3 cents per kilowatt. Construction began
  16|                          in November 2016 and is expected to be complete by early spring 2017.
  17|                          The focus of school maintenance for FY17 continues to be preventative maintenance of heating and ventila-
  18|                          tion systems and energy conservation measures. In addition, the maintenance staff began training on the
  19|                          building systems of the new school in July. Conversion of existing interior lighting to LED at Lunenburg
  20|                          Primary School began in December 2016 and will complete in March 2017. Funding for conversion of Tur-
  21|                          key Hill Elementary School will be a high priority in the upcoming Green Communities Competitive Grant
  22|                          rounds.
  23|                          The third grade was relocated to Turkey Hill, which was re-designated an elementary school, in June 2016.
  24|                           To be better able to support grades 3-5, a playground was added to the side of the school by the generous do-
  25|                          nation of the Connor Lourens Memorial Fund, the BEIM Foundation c/o Erik and Megan Arnold, the Turkey
  26|                          Hill Family Lions Club, the Lunenburg PTO, Extended Day Program, parents, guardians, and citizens. The
  27|                          playground was dedicated in September 2016 in memory of Jane Doyle.
  28|                          The FY 17 capital program funded replacement of the Turkey Hill Elementary School phone system and in-
  29|                          tercom system at $22,000 each. The new systems were installed by Vertical Communications Inc. in July
  30|                          2016. For the FY18 Capital Program, the School Department has requested an additional $145,000 funding
  31|                          for continued asbestos removal at Turkey Hill Elementary School as its highest priority. Additionally the
  32|                           district is looking to add a security camera system to Turkey Hill Elementary School and Lunenburg Primary
  33|                          School during FY17 using funds that are anticipated to be surplus to the operating budget due to reduced cost
  34|                          for natural gas and electricity and the impact of the solar projects.
  35|                          Respectfully submitted,
  36|                          John Londa, Director of Facilities and Grounds
  37|                                                                                                    103

===PAGE 104=== (ocr, 35 lines)
   1|                                                                                   Technology Department
   2|                           It has been a great pleasure to oversee the stunning technology improvements to the district over the past
   3|                           year. Bringing the new Lunenburg Middle-High School online has provided not only a major upgrade to
   4|                           technology for our sixth through twelfth grade classes, but has afforded us the opportunity to expand access
   5|                           to devices and improve infrastructure at the Primary and Elementary schools as well. IPad carts, Wi-Fi ac-
   6|                           cess points, desktops, laptops, printers and other technology were redistributed from the previous Lunenburg
   7|                           High School to the Primary and Elementary schools, improving each of those areas by as much as fifty per-
   8|                           cent.
   9|                           The new Middle-High School is home to state of the art technology that is pervasive throughout the building.
  10|                           • Interactive projectors and voice-lift audio systems are in all classrooms and collaborative workspaces.
  11|                           • Grades ten through twelve are participating in a one-to-one Chromebook initiative, putting a device in
  12|                                 each student's hands for use in class and at home.
  13|                           • Computer labs, both mobile and static, comprise more than two hundred additional devices for use on the
  14|                                premises.
  15|                          • A high capacity Wi-Fi system with more than one hundred access points provides ubiquitous, secure ac-
  16|                                 cess to the network for students, faculty, and staff - even allowing for multiple devices per person.
  17|                           • A beautiful new auditorium space with professionally designed sound, lights and rigging is available for
  18|                                 use not only by students and school personnel but for town functions and outside productions and events
  19|                                 as well.
  20|                           The newly named Turkey Hill Elementary School has seen some significant technology improvements this
  21|                           past year. The number of Wi-Fi access points was increased by fifty percent, improving coverage and capac-
  22|                           ity throughout the building. Additional iPad and Chromebook carts have been deployed, some from the pre-
  23|                           vious Lunenburg High School and some donated by extremely generous individuals and organizations. Ad-
  24|                           ditional multimedia carts were also deployed providing projection and audio capabilities to more classrooms.
  25|                           Lunenburg Primary School also benefited from the closing of LHS. Again, we were able to increase the
  26|                           number of Wi-Fi access points by fifty percent, deploy additional mobile computer labs, replace outdated
  27|                           classroom computers, and increase the availability of multimedia carts.
  28|                           The Technology Department is also proud to have added an additional full-time staff member, our School
  29|                           Technology Coordinator Nick Scoggins. Our goal is always to use technology to enhance our students'
  30|                           learning experience, and to get that technology into as many students' hands as possible. This past year has
  31|                           provided us with an opportunity to do that on a grander scale than ever before.
  32|                           Respectfully submitted,
  33|                           Steve Malandrinos
  34|                           Technology Director
  35|                                                                                                    104

   1|                                                                               Special Services Department
   2|                                                                 FY16 (data taken from 2015-2016 school year)
   3|                                     From the March 1, 2016 report, there are two hundred and ninety nine students with disabilities re-
   4|                          ceiving special education or related services in Lunenburg. This represents 18.8% of our total student popula-
   5|                          tion of 1,587 students (it was 17.2% last year). The state average from the 2016 school report card is 17.2%.
   6|                          As of March 1, 2016, the total number of students receiving services outside the district was nineteen (last
   7|                         year was thirty). Of the nineteen students, four are placed in Collaborative Placements, twelve are in Day
   8|                          Placements and three are in Residential Placements. Additionally, as of January 12, 2017, one hundred four-
   9|                         teen students identified with a disability received accommodations via 504 plans in Lunenburg.
  10|                                     This year as a district, our overall Accountability and Assistance Level is Level 2 with the Primary
  11|                          School, Turkey Hill Middle School, and Lunenburg High School all being listed as Level 2 (LHS was Level 1
  12|                         last year). The state report card lists the district's determination of need for Special Education technical assis-
  13|                         tance or intervention for Students with Disabilities in Lunenburg as "Meets Requirements - At Risk
  14|                          (MRAR)". The district's Students with Disabilities progress towards narrowing proficiency gaps [Cumulative
  15|                          Progress and Performance Index (CPI): 1-100)] is listed at 45 (it was 46 last year) with the target set for 75.
  16|                          Students with Disabilities participated in MCAS this year with the following rates: English Language Arts =
  17|                          99% (it was 99% last year), Mathematics = 100% (It was 99% last year), and Science = 100% (It was 100%
  18|                          last year).
  19|                          This data can be found at the DESE website: http://profiles.doe.mass.edu/accountability/report/district.aspx?
  20|                          linkid=30&orgcode=01620000&orgtypecode=5&
  21|                                     Other data collected throughout the year is also important to consider when looking at the whole Spe-
  22|                          cial Services Department. Department of Elementary and Secondary Education requires us to collect data for
  23|                          certain Indicators they are targeting each year. For our Indicators, the following information is available be-
  24|                         low and also can be found at: http://profiles.doe.mass.edu/gis/sped_map.aspx? orgcode=01620000&
  25|                          Indicator 1 - Graduation Rate. For the 2014-2015 school year, the state target for Students with Disabilities
  26|                          was 82.0%. Lunenburg's rate was 66.7% and the overall state rate was 69.9% for Students with Disabilities.
  27|                          Indicator 2 - Dropout Rate. For the 2014-2015 school year the state target for Students with Disabilities was
  28|                          3.0%. Lunenburg rate for Students with IEPs was 0% (it was 2.2% last year) and the overall state rate was
  29|                          2.9%.
  30|                          Indicator 3C - Proficiency Rate for Students with IEPs on MCAS. For the 2015 MCAS, Lunenburg Stu-
  31|                          dents with Disabilities CPI ratings were as follows:

   1|                          Indicator 4 - Suspension (more than 10 days)/Expulsion for Students with IEPs. For Lunenburg, our rate
   2|                          was 0% (last year it was .7%. The state rate was 1.1%. Additionally, Indicator 4B records significant dis-
   3|                          crepancies with the Suspension/Expulsion rates by race or ethnicity. In 2015, Lunenburg was not found to
   4|                          have any significant discrepancies due to these factors.
   5|                          Indicator 5 - Educational Environments for Students Aged 6-21 with IEPs. For 2014-2015, the state's target
   6|                          rates are as follows: for Full Inclusion of students = 60.5%, Partial Inclusion = N/A, Substantially Separate
   7|                          Placements = 14.5%, and for Separate Schools/Residential Facilities/Homebound/Hospitals = 5.5%. In
   8|                          Lunenburg, our percentages are as follows: Full Inclusion = 69.6, Partial = 13.2%, Substantially Separate =
   9|                          7.0% and Separate schools, Residential, etc. = 10.1%.
  10|                          Indicator 6 - Educational Environments for Students Aged 3-5 with IEPs. The 2014-2015 state rates
  11|                          showed the percent of students receiving a majority of their special education and related services in a Full
  12|                          Inclusion early childhood program was 41.0%, in a Partial Inclusion Program was N/A, a Substantially Sepa-
  13|                          rate Placement (special education class, separate school, or residential facility) was 13.5%, and students not
  14|                          attending an early childhood program but receiving only a related service (e.g. speech, OT, PT) was N/A. In
  15|                          Lunenburg, our early childhood Full Inclusion rate was 32.4%, our Partial Inclusion rate was 44.1%, Substan-
  16|                          tially Separate rate was 17.6%, and Related Services was 5.9%.
  17|                          Indicator 8 - Parent Involvement. This indicator measures the % of parents with a child receiving special
  18|                          education who report that school facilitated parent involvement as a means of improving services and results
  19|                          for students with IEPs. For 2014-2015 the state target was 85%. Lunenburg's rate was 78.3% and the state's
  20|                          rate was 79.7% (from information collected in the spring of 2013).
  21|                          Indicator 9 & 10 - Both of these Indicators deal with Disproportionality in Special Education and in Special
  22|                          Education Categories. In Lunenburg the state determined that there was no disproportionality found in either
  23|                          case.
  24|                          Indicator 11 - Initial Evaluation Timelines. This indicator measures the % of children with parental consent
  25|                          to evaluate, who were evaluated within the State established timeline. Data is reported for all initial evalua-
  26|                          tions initiated in October, November, and December of the reporting year. Lunenburg was rated 100% in
  27|                          compliance and the state average for this Indicator was 98.7%.
  28|                          Indicator 12 - Early Childhood Transition. This indicator measures the % of children referred by Part C,
  29|                          found eligible for special education services, and who have an IEP developed and implemented by their third
  30|                          birthdays. The state target for this indicator in 100% and Lunenburg is noted as "Suppressed". Data is sup-
  31|                          pressed if the number of students referred by Part C and found eligible for special education services is fewer
  32|                          than 10. In Lunenburg, only 2 students were referred during 2012-2013 (we have not been reviewed since
  33|                          2012-2013 for updated data in this area.
  34|                          Indicator 13 - Secondary Transition. This indicator measures the % of youth with IEPs aged 16 and above
  35|                          with an IEP that includes appropriate measurable postsecondary goals that are annually updated and based
  36|                          upon an age appropriate transition assessment, transition services, including courses of study, that will rea-
  37|                          sonably enable the student to meet those postsecondary goals, and annual IEP goals related to the student's
  38|                          transition service needs. Lunenburg was rated 100% in compliance and the state average for this Indicator
  39|                          was 99.2% (we have not been reviewed since 2011-2012 for updated data in this area.
  40|                          Indicator 14 - Post-School Outcomes for Students with IEPs. This indicator measures the % of students
  41|                          with IEPs who exited high school during the 2013-14 school year and self-reported post-school engagement
  42|                          in education or employment one year after leaving high school. Engagement is defined through three meas-
  43|                          urements:
  44|                          • Measurement A: Enrolled in higher education within one year of leaving high school.
  45|                          • Measurement B: Enrolled in higher education or competitively employed within one year of leaving high
  46|                          • Measurement C: Enrolled in higher education, or in some other postsecondary education or training pro-
  47|                          gram; or competitively employed, or in some other employment within one year of leaving high school.
  48|                          Measurements are cumulative with Measurement C reflecting the total number of exiters engaged in a post-
  49|                          secondary activity one year after leaving high school. For the 2014-2015 school year state targets for the
  50|                          three measurements of Post-School Outcomes are Measurement A: 47%; Measurement B: 82%; and
  51|                                                                                                    106

   1|                            Measurement C: 89%. Lunenburg rates are as follows: Measurement A = 50%, Measurement B = 75% and
   2|                            Measurement C = 75%.
   3|                                       The Parent Advisory Council Supporting All Learners (PACSAL) continues to be an active support
   4|                           group for parents, teachers and administration. All parents and community members can join the group,
   5|                           which currently consists of parents of students receiving Title I services and parents of students with disabil-
   6|                           ities. Meetings are typically held the first Friday of each month, 11:00AM, in the Planning Room at the
   7|                            Middle School. The executive board is made up of Chairperson: Kelly Harvey. Several events and presenta-
   8|                           tions were organized by the PACSAL for both parents and teachers in Lunenburg this year. For more infor-
   9|                           mation, go to: https://sites.google.com/a/lunenburgschools.net/special-services/pacsal
  10|                                       In conclusion, the Lunenburg Public Schools continues to provide a variety of special needs services
  11|                           to meet the varying needs of our students in the least restrictive environment. We have an experienced,
  12|                           hardworking staff that works together with parents to provide these varied services. Our parents are equal
  13|                           partners in our efforts to develop challenging and focused educational programs for children. We remain
  14|                           committed to the Lunenburg Public Schools mission, "We prepare our students for lifelong learning and re-
  15|                           sponsible community membership." This is a total community effort and we encourage ALL Lunenburg
  16|                           residents to join with us to create positive experiences and futures for our children.
  17|                                      For additional information, please visit our website at: https://sites.google.com/a/
  18|                           lunenburgschools.net/special-services/, or contact Julianna Hanscom, Director of Special Services, Special
  19|                           Services Office, 1025 Massachusetts Ave, (978) 582-4100, ext. 305.

  21|                           Julianna Hanscom

  23|                                                                          Lunenburg School Lunch Program
  24|                                                                                              2016-2017
  25|                           The Food Service Director Nadine Lorenzen and the program managers Patti Pichnarcik at Lunenburg Mid-
  26|                           dle-High School, Joslin Schneider at Turkey Hill Elementary School and Claudette Lachance at the Primary
  27|                           School would like to highlight some of the lunch programs endeavors started this year beginning with the

  29|                           Houses was a great opportunity for parents to sample menu items served in our cafeterias. The menus for all
