===PAGE 108=== (ocr, 50 lines)
   1|                                                                        LUNENBURG PRIMARY SCHOOL
   2|                                      The Lunenburg Primary School is an early childhood education center for students Preschool to 2nd
   3|                          grade. Our students range from three to eight years old. Our community is made up of students, parents,
   4|                          teachers and support staff; working together to create an educationally rich and safe learning environment that
   5|                          focuses on the whole child.
   6|                                      The October 1, 2016 student count reported to the Department of Elementary and Secondary Educa-
   7|                          tion was three hundred and eighty-six students. Our Preschool program services forty-one three and four year
   8|                           old students. We have five Kindergarten classes servicing one hundred and eighteen students. Class sizes in
   9|                          our Kindergartens are at 23 to 24 students. First grade has one hundred and eleven students with class sizes
  10|                          ranging from 22 to 23. Second grade has one hundred and sixteen students with class sizes ranging from 23 to
  11|                          24 students. Students and staff this year are enjoying the extra space produced when the third grade students
  12|                          and staff were moved to the Turkey Hill Elementary School as a result of the new Lunenburg Middle-High
  13|                          School build.
  14|                                      Our professional development this year centered on Everyday Math Online Resources, Responsive
  15|                          Classroom and Emotional Intelligence. All of our classroom teachers are working on improving the use of
  16|                          Responsive Classroom Learning Curriculum which includes morning meetings, guided discovery, rules, logi-
  17|                          cal consequences and academic choice. Two years ago we updated our math curriculum and materials to
  18|                          Every Day Math. Teachers have been involved in two years of professional development activities focused
  19|                          on lesson structure, math games and developing ways to incorporate technology into their teaching of math.
  20|                          This year students and parents were given access to the online resources as well so that students and parents
  21|                          have a place to explore math concepts and access games on line to practice math skills.
  22|                                      To strengthen our commitment to focusing on the whole child and the Social Emotional Learning
  23|                          (SEL) needs of our children, teachers and staff have received professional development on Responsive class-
  24|                          room and Emotional Intelligence. Mrs. Nancy Chapdelaine has led our educators through in-service lessons
  25|                          dedicated to Understanding the Power of Our Words and Morning Meeting structures and strategies. After
  26|                          attending the Yale Institutes RULER Training, Mrs. Dorsey; School Psychologist, Mrs. Favreau; 15 Grade
  27|                          Teacher and Mrs. Blaisdell, Principal, led teachers through completing PD hours focused on Emotional Intel-
  28|                          ligence using RULER. The PD activities have focused just on the staff understanding RULER and learning
  29|                          how to utilize the tools to understand their own emotions and how they affect our work together with children.
  30|                           This is the beginning of a three to four year commitment to making the Lunenburg Primary School one that
  31|                          focuses on helping our community of students, staff and parents understand their emotions and how we bring
  32|                           our best selves to the learning we do each and every day. Information on these two SEL's can be found at
  33|                          https://www.responsiveclassroom.org/and http://ei.yale.edu/
  34|                                      The Primary School is a Title One school and we have continued our tiered instructional system to
  35|                          insure that all students have the services they need to be successful. Common assessments are completed in
  36|                          the fall, winter and spring for all children in grades K-3 in the areas of reading, writing, and math. These
  37|                          common assessments serve to monitor the effectiveness of teacher instruction and drive individual student
  38|                          instruction. Struggling students are identified quickly and additional resources and instruction are provided to
  39|                          address the areas of concern identified. Tiered instructional services are provided in the area of Language,
  40|                           Arts and Math. The tiered instructional model requires that the teachers not only meet the needs of the stu-
  41|                          dents who may be struggling with learning but those students who are doing well and need to achieve higher
  42|                          levels of learning. Learning is more personalized and students are being challenged to be confident independ-
  43|                          ent learners and problem solvers.
  44|                           Our staff works hard to maintain a school culture which is positive, inclusive, and supportive of each of our
  45|                          members. We have high expectations, insist on respectful behavior, and recognize the importance of building
  46|                          a community where members feel a sense of belonging and responsibility to contribute. Our students have
  47|                          maintained a high level of community engagement and civic learning that is fostered through service learning
  48|                          projects at each grade level. The Kindergarten students learn about our local food bank and gift mart and
  49|                          sponsor school-wide can and gift drives through-out the year. Our
  50|                                                                                                    108

===PAGE 109=== (ocr, 21 lines)
   1|                                          grade students have learned about our local veterans and organized Valentines to go to area Vet-
   2|                           eran's Hospitals. Students in the First grade have committed their energy to support our local animal shel-
   3|                                      The Primary School Parent/Teacher Advisory Council meets each month to discuss school issues,
   4|                          review the annual budget for the school and advise administration on the school's parent handbook and to
   5|                           develop the School Improvement Plan.
   6|                           Members of the Council for the 2016-2017 school year were as follows:

  12|                           School Committee Rep:                          Dawn-Marie Ayles
  13|                                     The faculty and staff of the Primary School are extremely proud of the work we do with our children.
  14|                           We are deeply committed to student learning, our own life-long learning and improving learning. Only
  15|                           through working with parents as partners are we able to accomplish our goals. For more information about
  16|                          the work that we do at the Lunenburg Primary School please visit our website: https://sites.google.com/a/

   1|                                                                         TURKEY HILL ELEMENTARY SCHOOL
   2|                         With the completion of the new Middle High school, Turkey Hill has been reconfigured as an elementary
   3|                         school for students in grades 3 -5. The staff includes a number of skilled veterans who have been reunited from
   4|                         T.C. Passios days as well as many young and energetic new educators. We are focused on developing our own
   5|                         distinctive perspective as to how we best prepare students for lifelong learning and responsible community
   6|                         membership.
   7|                         Our academic priority is to increase student learning and improve students' ability to think critically and
   8|                         achieve at high levels. The school advisory council has grappled with how we might best reflect progress par-
   9|                         ticularly focusing this year on math, using MCAS data. We agreed to reflect growth using three measures:
  10|                         • Increase by 3 points annually the CPI score in Math
  11|                         • Increase to 70% the % of Proficient or Higher in Math
  12|                         • Attain a two year average of 50% SGP in Math by the end of 5' grade

===PAGE 112=== (ocr, 52 lines)
   1|                         For some staff, professional development has focused on the Everyday Math Program, as the most recent ver-
   2|                         sion has radically altered the scope and sequence of curriculum to more accurately reflect the demands of the
   3|                         Common Core. Other teachers have taken on extra responsibilities as part of the ELA, Math, and Science task
   4|                         forces, in order to create comprehension curriculum maps for grades K-12. We have embraced and continue
   5|                        to refine the Teacher Support Team protocol, which examines struggling students' academic/personal histo-
   6|                         ries and focuses on the best interventions. Our aim is not only to support a teacher's current concerns, but also
   7|                         to streamline access to important background information and most successful strategies for next year's teach-
   8|                         ers. Staff is learning to access Edwin Analytics, the Dept. of Education's data warehouse, so they might ana-
   9|                        lyze the individual MCAS scores of their current students and more precisely respond to areas of strength or
  10|                         weakness. All students were assessed in October and will be again in May using the Developmental Reading
  11|                         Assessment (DRA). It is a designed to help teachers systematically observe, record, and evaluate changes in
  12|                         student reading performance and features tools to assess reading engagement, fluency, and comprehension.
  13|                         We are fortunate to have the services of a full time reading specialist, who works with Title 1 students and
  14|                         provides support and consultation for classroom teachers. Finally, we also have a wide variety of academical-
  15|                        ly themed field trips planned, both out in the community and several "in-house" science and fine arts pro-
  16|                         grams.
  17|                         We acknowledge that social learning in a supportive community is as important to success as academic learn-
  18|                        ing. To that end, grades 3 and 4 use the Second Step guidance curriculum, and grade 5 has developed a set of
  19|                        lessons that emphasize executive functioning skills. With the full time services of our guidance counselor,
  20|                         many students meet weekly in small groups to develop both social skills and strategies to cope with anxiety,
  21|                         focus issues, and academic challenges. We facilitate the opportunity for outside counselors to meet with stu-
  22|                         dents one to one. We host the Big Sibs, Little Sibs program on Wednesday afternoons for high school students
  23|                         to make connections with younger students in a structured activity group. We offer a variety of after school
  24|                         activities so students might continue to develop their social adeptness and sense of self confidence. We have
  25|                         built time into our faculty meetings for teachers to send every single student a postcard once during the year,
  26|                         celebrating their strengths, developing skills, or positive attributes. We hosted the TIGER program from
  27|                         Plymouth State University that focuses on a positive message of inclusiveness and how to advocate for your-
  28|                         self and others. We have developed a set of standard expectations for hallway behavior, playground behavior,
  29|                         and cafeteria behavior.
  30|                         We are engaged in this transition with a foundation of open communication and sound operational practices.
  31|                         Our leadership team consists of reps from each grade level, special areas, and special services. We are focused
  32|                         on developing the attributes of a highly effective team, as our ultimate goal is to model those attributes and
  33|                         see them mirrored in every other "team" throughout the building. We place strong value on turn-taking, empa-
  34|                         thy, embracing the difficult conversations, and psychological safety. The members are both conduits of com-
  35|                         munication between administration and staff and decision makers about the direction of our school's culture
  36|                         and priorities. Likewise, the School Advisory Council, composed of parents, community members, teachers
  37|                         and a school committee representative, also provide input and influence the school's culture and priorities.
  38|                         The principal's role as instructional leader is part of the equation as well. Providing effective feedback to
  39|                        teachers around the teaching and learning is an essential element for student success.
  40|                          Education is a community partnership. Ultimately, THES does not stand in isolation. It is the sustenance of
  41|                         the larger community that enriches the learning opportunities for students and staff. The list of supporters is
  42|                         long and likely incomplete: the PTO whose funds support a huge variety of endeavors, the Lunenburg Lions
  43|                          Club who has been so instrumental in the creation of our beautiful playground, an anonymous donor who gift-
  44|                         ed an entire Chromebook cart, BoxTop collectors and counters, Hannaford Helps supporters, Community
  45|                          Reader day volunteers, parents (both those who support their children at home as well as those who donate
  46|                         hours to school), the Rebecca's Lodge who donated dictionaries to third grade, the Lunenburg Cultural Coun-
  47|                         cil whose grant makes it possible for our African Arts in Education program, our Veteran's Day and Memorial
  48|                          Day guests and guest speakers. Their selfless efforts exemplify what we strive to imbue in our children: re-
  49|                         sponsible community membership.
  50|                         Respectfully Submitted,
  51|                         Heidi Champagne, Principal

  14|                                                                                 Lunenburg Middle School
  15|                           Lunenburg Middle School provides a rigorous academic curriculum as well as strong programs that support
  16|                           students' social and emotional growth. These programs ensure that each child becomes a self-motivated,
  17|                           creative, lifelong learner in a safe, stimulating environment that encourages diversity and open honest com-
  18|                           munication. The dedicated and accomplished staff of professionals within the school embraces the philos-
  19|                           ophy of educating the whole child academically, socially, and emotionally. This philosophy is a means by
  20|                           which all students become positive community members that possess the skills to be successful in high
  21|                           school. Lunenburg Middle School is in its first year as a co-located middle- high school serving grades six
  22|                           through eight in a beautiful new facility. Middle school students have experienced some new opportunities
  23|                           with the new middle school, such as foreign language for grades seven and eight and increased technology
  24|                           instruction from a dedicated technology teacher. Grade six students have an increase in special area oppor-
  25|                           tunities, such as health, art music and guidance. We will continue to seek out opportunities for students that
  26|                           foster critical thinking and problem solving skills that prepare them for high school and beyond.
  27|                           In reviewing the results of the 2016 Spring M.C.A.S. tests, our percentage of students scoring in the ad-
  28|                           vanced and proficient range remains strong. The Department of Elementary and Secondary Education did
  29|                           not provide 2016 state percentages of students scoring in the proficient and advanced range due to a number
  30|                           of districts participating in the PARCC assessments. State percentages from 2015 have been provided mere-
  31|                           ly as a reference. In five of the six content areas that have student growth measured, Lunenburg Middle
  32|                           School students performed in the 50% or higher as a grade level median, indicating average to above aver-
  33|                           age student growth. Although we are proud of our students' performance, we continue to analyze data in
  34|                           our monthly results faculty meetings designed to improve our instruction for all students. Our faculty has
  35|                           identified areas of improvement based upon the data and implemented strategies to improve their instruc-
  36|                           tional practices that will help students succeed.
  37|                           This is the first year in four years in which the eighth grade has returned to the traditional middle school
  38|                           model from the high school. As the eighth grade transitions back to a true middle school model, we reflect
  39|                                                                                                    114

===PAGE 115=== (ocr, 37 lines)
   1|                         on strategies that have been successful with students they experienced in the high school setting, while continu-
   2|                        ing to incorporate the foundations of a successful middle school. This integration of strategies continues to have
   3|                         a positive impact on the students we serve and we will continue to refine and implement what is best for stu-
   4|                         We continue to utilize intervention and power blocks during the school day to meet the needs of each individual
   5|                         student. These blocks of instructional time that have been built into our master schedule allow teachers time to
   6|                        work with students at their own individual skill level. The philosophy is to challenge students who are high
   7|                        academic achievers as well as strengthen skills for students who have identified weaknesses. Teachers are able
   8|                        to work with students in a small group to answer individual questions or provide more individualized support.
   9|                        We design this support model to allow students to feel more confident, thus, perform better in the classroom.
  10|                         We will continue to refine our intervention block times to best meet the needs of our students.
  11|                        Lunenburg Middle School continues to offer enrichment blocks for all students. Enrichment classes provide
  12|                        students a block of time in which they receive instruction in the areas of developmental health and guidance.
  13|                        Students also receive instruction in physical education, art, and music classes that allows them to explore their
  14|                         strengths and talents. Students also have the opportunity to sign up for and participate in band and chorus dur-
  15|                        ing this block. This enrichment block is in addition to the traditional special area classes students receive, and
  16|                        this block offers students additional physical movement throughout the day, additional opportunities for hands
  17|                        on activities and more exposure to the arts, theatre and technology throughout the school day.
  18|                        We make a positive impact on the students we serve by offering a variety of different programs developed to
  19|                         promote social and emotional growth, self-awareness, and community service. We will continue to utilize De-
  20|                         velopmental Design and Advisory programs to deliver our social emotional curriculum. Students meet with
  21|                        teachers in small groups to teach social responsibility and problem solving, as well as developing empathy and
  22|                         promoting positive self-esteem. Our seventh grade students are participating in themes such as embracing and
  23|                        respecting diversity. All of the social emotional programs have been developed to allow students to showcase
  24|                         their talents and abilities, grow and improve as individuals, and promote community outreach for the younger
  25|                         generation.
  26|                         This year, we have been able to offer foreign language back to our seventh and eighth grades for the first time
  27|                        in several years. The addition of the foreign language instruction has been well received and the students enjoy
  28|                        exploring different cultures and languages.
  29|                         Lunenburg Middle School continues to provide educational services and support that ensures student success
  30|                         and promotes lifelong learning and responsible community membership. The key components to our success is
  31|                         our talented faculty committed to making a positive impact on students, our parents and community who sup-
  32|                         port our teaching and learning, and most of all, our students who inspire us through their commitment to learn-
  33|                         ing. Together we continue to make the difference in the lives of students.
  34|                        Respectfully submitted,
  35|                        Tim Santry
  36|                        Principal

===PAGE 117=== (ocr, 44 lines)
   1|                                                                                   Lunenburg High School
   2|                           Lunenburg High School has been a hub for academic and social activity in 2016-2017. We have made the
   3|                           transition to the new Lunenburg Middle-High School. The transition process was far bigger than just mov-
   4|                          ing from one building to another. With the middle and high school in the same building, there have been far
   5|                           more opportunities than challenges. High school students have been positive influences on the middle
   6|                          school students and the staffs of the middle and high schools now have the chance to engage in vertical team-
   7|                           ing. All students in grades 10-12 have been issued a Chrome Book and there are wireless capabilities
   8|                          throughout the building. Every classroom and instructional space has an interactive projector and voice lift
   9|                           capabilities. Our students are fortunate to be in a building that can meet the needs of the 21 century learner.
  10|                           This year, we have gone back to the traditional high school model. Grades 9-12 are part of the high school
  11|                          and grades 6-8 are part of the middle school. Besides the bus ride into school, there is very limited contact
  12|                          between high school and middle school students. We have two lunches comprised of high school students
  13|                          and two lunches with only middle school students. Additionally, the building was designed to have separate
  14|                          locker rooms for middle and high school students. Overall, there have been very few issues with keeping
  15|                          the schools separate. As a matter of fact, we will likely look for more opportunities to bring the students to-
  16|                          gether, in the future.
  17|                           NEASC ACCREDITATION
  18|                           Lunenburg High School completed a Special Progress Report for the Commission on Public Secondary
  19|                           Schools of the New England Association of Schools and Colleges. In 2010, the Commission recommended
  20|                          that the school receive continued accreditation in the Association. The Commission's decision was based up-
  21|                          on review of an evaluation report prepared by a visiting committee in April 2010. The Two Year Progress
  22|                           Report, submitted in January, 2013 received high marks from the Commission reviewing our submission.
  23|                           The Mid-Cycle Progress Report was reviewed and accepted by the Commission. A Special Progress Report
  24|                           was filed in September to certify that the building project had been completed. Lunenburg High School has
  25|                           been taken off "Warning" status and given full accreditation.
  26|                           SCHOOL/COMMUNITY PARTNERSHIPS
  27|                           Throughout the year, Lunenburg High School students are involved in a multitude of fundraising and collec-
  28|                           tion endeavors. In the fall, a canned goods drive was held and admission to the PowderPuff game was two
  29|                          canned goods. Everything collected was donated to the Lunenburg Food Pantry. Around the holiday season,
  30|                          advisories collected items for the Lunenburg Gift Mart.
  31|                           There is a Senior English class running this year that has a community service learning component. Students
  32|                           were tasked with researching and identifying needs in the community and then forming an action plan to ad-
  33|                           dress the needs. For example, we have students working on: a pet shelter drive, creating trail maps for
  34|                           Lunenburg conservation land, bringing a small breakfast program to LHS, and forming a group to inform
  35|                          and educate about mental health issues.
  36|                           Lunenburg High School received a generous donation in the amount of $500 from Mr. Mike's Mobil in
  37|                           Lunenburg, MA. The grant is to be used toward STEM initiatives at the high school. We are in the process
  38|                          of purchasing a new robot for our Robotics class.
  39|                           Hannaford Helps Schools made a generous donation to Lunenburg High School of $500 from receipt sales.
  40|                          This money will be used to benefit a variety of programs at LHS.
  41|                          In March, the National Honor Society will be having its annual Blood Drive. Students sixteen and over (and
  42|                           staff members) will be able to participate in the Blood Drive with parent permission.
  43|                           LHS has held evening presentations for parents/guardians on the following topics:
  44|                                                                                                    117

   9|                         MCAS RESULTS
  10|                         We are pleased to announce that Lunenburg High School is recognized by the Department of Elementary and
  11|                         Secondary Education as a Level 2 school. As a Level 2 school, we continue to be among the highest pertorm-
  12|                        ing schools in the Commonwealth, but shows that we are not meeting our gap narrowing goals. While we are
  13|                        recognized as a high performing school, we understand that there is work to be done in showing growth with
  14|                         our high needs population.

  27|                         them at the top 25 percent of their district. To be eligible, students must either score Advanced on one exam

  30|                         Homecoming was once again a great town-wide celebration, culminating with a beautiful parade complete with

  32|                         and we were able to play on our new turf field. For much of the fall season, our home games needed to be
  33|                         played off-site (i.e. Doyle Field, Progin Park in Lancaster, and Marshall Park.) Many of our sports teams quali-
  34|                         fied for tournament and district play. The LHS Mock Trial team has performed well this year, going 1-1 in lo-
  35|                         cal trials. The Latin Club, marching band, jazz band and the math team represented LHS in numerous competi-

===PAGE 119=== (ocr, 41 lines)
   1|                         In December, we brought the SHINE Initiative, to LHS. Three members of the SHINE Initiative gave
   2|                          presentations to students in all grade levels. The presentations were about 45 minutes long. The SHINE Ini-
   3|                          tiative focuses on the mental health and wellness of children and young adults. They raise awareness of
   4|                          mental illness and help to defuse and debunk some of the misconceptions and myths that have fueled stigma
   5|                          for generations. In January, we hosted a community presentation.
   6|                          We started an Advisory program two years ago. One goal of Advisory is to have students have at least one
   7|                          adult who will get to know him/her over the course of four years. Students were randomly distributed into
   8|                         small grade-level groups. They will stay with the same group of students, as well. Another goal of Adviso-
   9|                          ry is to develop relationships with students of different backgrounds and social groups so that students can
  10|                          get to know each other and develop an appreciation of the diversity that we do have in Lunenburg. Some
  11|                          Advisories will be facilitating some community service learning projects.

===PAGE 122=== (ocr, 52 lines)
   1|                                     MONTACHUSETT REGIONAL TECHNICAL VOCATIONAL SCHOOL
   2|                                                             SUPERINTENDENT- DIRECTOR REPORT
   3|                         2015-2016 was an exceptional year at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District. We
   4|                         celebrated "50 years of Excellence in Education," and through a thoughtful and reflective marketing campaign,
   5|                         we were able to honor the school's history and highlight the successes of our proud alumni and notable
   6|                         achievements of our current students. We launched a robust technology initiative, providing all teachers with
   7|                        technology and training in preparation for a whole-school Chromebook rollout in FY17. Talented students,
   8|                        faculty and staff earned countless awards and recognitions, making our district so very proud. And finally, the
   9|                         school began efforts to bring our 21% career and technical education program to the school - Veterinary
  10|                        Science. This work would not be possible without the contributions of the many talented educators and
  11|                         administrators here at Monty Tech - a team I am honored to lead in my role as Superintendent-Director. I am
  12|                         delighted to present the District's 2015-2016 annual report to you, providing a snapshot of the wonderful
  13|                         experiences happening on a day-to-day basis here at Monty Tech, highlighting some of the year's most notable
  14|                         achievements, including:
  15|                        • Efforts to bring the school's 215* vocational-technical program are well underway. After a careful review
  16|                              of workforce projections and student interest surveys, school administrators have begun the process to
  17|                              bring a Chapter 74 Animal Science program to the district, which will be open to students Fall 2017. This
  18|                              exciting STEM program will be housed in a state-of-the-art facility that boasts a classroom, science lab,
  19|                              grooming salon, and full-service veterinary clinic. In an unprecedented fundraising campaign, school
  20|                              administrators have raised an impressive $1,975,461 in donations and in-kind matching services to support
  21|                              this project. With an overall cost of $2,250,000, construction of the Monty Tech Veterinary Science
  22|                               Training Center and Community Clinic is in progress, providing tremendous hands-on learning
  23|                              opportunities to students in our Carpentry, Plumbing, Electrical, Cabinetmaking, HVAC, Masonry, and
  24|                              Welding trades.
  25|                           A unique partnership with Workers' Credit Union has resulted in a full-service branch located on the
  26|                              school's campus, and increased opportunities for students to develop authentic financial literacy skills
  27|                              while still in high school. Students studying in the school's Business Technology program are being
  28|                              trained as bank tellers, preparing them for entry-level jobs in banking institutions across North Central
  29|                               Massachusetts, and financial literacy workshops are being integrated into the curriculum each year.
  30|                           Senior students in the school's very popular Health Occupations program participated in the first year of
  31|                              an exciting new partnership between Monty Tech and Mount Wachusett Community College. This
  32|                              innovative high school-to-college collaboration, which embeds a comprehensive, college-level Emergency
  33|                               Medical Technician (EMT) training program into the senior year of study has resulted in a number of
  34|                              students earning EMT credentials, providing additional career pathways in the medical field for these
  35|                              talented program graduates.
  36|                        • Students at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School continued to demonstrate high academic
  37|                              achievement. In the spring of 2016, Monty Tech's passing rate on the MCAS English Language Arts
  38|                              exam was 100%, Mathematics 99%, and Biology 99%.
  39|                        We are so proud of the educational programs offered here at Monty Tech, yet we continually strive to improve
  40|                        upon them. Collaborating with area colleges and universities, we are ensuring our curriculum and instruction
  41|                        are rigorous and relevant. Sharing best practices with vocational-technical colleagues from across the state -
  42|                        and nation - we give and take some of the best ideas, with one thing in mind - what is best for our students.
  43|                         And what is best, I believe, is preparing students for both college and career.
  44|                         We hope you will find this report a comprehensive review of the quality education you have come to know and
  45|                        expect from Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School. You may notice that each of the eighteen
  46|                        member cities and towns are reflected in this report, and that students performed services in almost every
  47|                        community last year. Providing our students with an opportunity to give back to the communities that support
  48|                         them - and support our school - is a pleasure.
  49|                                                                                                                                          Respectfully submitted,
  50|                                                                                                                                Sile In Harity
  51|                                                                                                        Sheila M. Harrity, Ed.D., Superintendent-Director
  52|                                                                                                    122

   1|                                                                                            Our Mission
   2|                         Every student will graduate from Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School with the skills,
   3|                         knowledge, and abilities to be a productive and effective member of an ever-changing society.
   4|                                                                                            Our District
   5|                         Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School is a four-year career and technical high school
   6|                         serving the member towns of:
   7|                                   Ashburnham                                              Harvard                                                  Princeton
   8|                                   Ashby                                                    Holden                                                  Royalston
   9|                                   Athol                                                    Hubbardston                                             Sterling
  10|                                   Barre                                                    Lunenburg                                               Templeton
  11|                                   Fitchburg                                                Petersham                                               Westminster
  12|                                   Gardner                                                 Phillipston                                              Winchendon
  13|                                                                                            Leadership
  14|                       The leadership team at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School is comprised of ten talented
  15|                       administrators whose varied educational backgrounds, professional experiences, and areas of expertise
  16|                       contribute to the success of the school. Working collaboratively, and under the direction of the
  17|                       Superintendent and Principal, the team has been able to transform the school into one of the most sought-
  18|                       after high schools in North Central Massachusetts.
  19|                       Sheila M. Harrity, Superintendent-Director                                           Christina Favreau, Director of Academic Programs
  20|                       Tom Browne, Principal                                                                Jim Hachey, Director of Vocational Programs
  21|                       Dayana Carlson, Assistant Principal                                                  Michael Gormley, Director of Facilities
  22|                       Tammy Crockett, Business Manager                                                     Katy Whitaker, Development Coordinator
  23|                       Pamela Pothier, Director of Technology                                               Victoria Zarozinski, Director of Student Support
  24|                                                                                                            Services
  25|                                                                                             Enrollment
  26|                          On June 1,2016, student enrollment at Monty Tech included 1,415 students in grades nine through
  27|                         twelve. Students are represented from every community in the district: Ashburnham (56), Ashby (32),
  28|                          Athol (85), Barre (37), Fitchburg (362), Gardner (143), Harvard (4), Holden (60), Hubbardston (70),
  29|                          Lunenburg (86), Petersham (3), Phillipston (19), Princeton (22), Royalston (18), Sterling (63), Templeton
  30|                         (103), Westminster (77), and Winchendon (150). The remaining 25 students were from out-of-district
  31|                         towns, including Ayer, Clinton, Dudley, Groton, Leominster, Orange, and Worcester.
  32|                          Throughout 2015-2016, Monty Tech offered a variety of opportunities for students, parents, and
  33|                          community members to learn about and visit the school. In October 2015, approximately 700 district
  34|                          eighth graders participated in the annual "Tour Day" event. Students toured our twenty vocational-
  35|                         technical areas and learned about the school's challenging academic offerings and exciting athletic and
  36|                          extracurricular programs. Career Awareness Night offered interested students the opportunity to return in
  37|                         the evening with their family members to further explore the facilities and talk with staff members.
  38|                          Each year, the Dean of Admissions conducts school visits, student interviews, and accepts applications
  39|                          for admissions. 2015-2016 proved to be an exceptionally busy year for her, as the school received 631
  40|                                                                                                    123

===PAGE 124=== (ocr, 44 lines)
   1|                         applications for admission. Of those, 575 were from students hoping to enter our incoming freshman class.
   2|                          The balance of the applications came from students hoping to enter the school as upperclassmen. Because
   3|                         there are only a limited number of students the school can accept each year, 338 freshmen and 3 upperclass-
   4|                          men were admitted.
   5|                          The Vocational Interest Program (V.I.P.) offers area seventh and eighth grade students the chance to visit
   6|                         Monty Tech after school and participate in hands-on learning experiences across a variety of vocational/
   7|                         technical areas. The program continued to attract a large number of students during the 2015-2016 school
   8|                         year, serving approximately 600 area students.
   9|                                                                                      Class of 2016 Awards
  10|                         Members of the Class of 2016 were awarded approximately $58,000 in scholarships. The Monty Tech Foun-
  11|                         dation generously provided $46,000 in scholarships to graduating seniors, ranging in amounts of $200 to
  12|                         $2,000. The Foundation also awarded $4,000 to the Practical Nursing graduates. Once again, local and state
  13|                          organizations, as well as generous individuals, continue to recognize the ability and potential of Monty Tech
  14|                         graduates in the form of financial donations. The School Committee, administration, faculty and graduates
  15|                         themselves are grateful for this support.
  16|                          Articulation Agreements with local colleges also play an important role in helping reduce the cost of higher
  17|                         education. Qualified Monty Tech students are eligible to receive college credits through a number of articu-
  18|                         lation agreements with public and private colleges across the country. Approximately 61% of the graduating
  19|                          class of 2016 reported plans to enroll at either a 2-year college, a 4-year college/university, or a technical/
  20|                         trade school upon graduation. By earning college credits while still in high school, these students will benefit
  21|                          by saving both time and money as they pursue advanced educational programs.
  22|                                                                                          Financial Report
  23|                         In an effort to develop a cost-effective budget for the fiscal year 2015-2016, a great deal of effort was put
  24|                         forth by the School Committee, administration, and staff. The final fiscal year 2015-2016 Educational Plan
  25|                         totaled $26,229,366, which represents a 2.3% increase over the 2014-2015 Educational Plan. The District's
  26|                          FY16 budget only exceeds the minimum spending required by Massachusetts General Law Chapter 70 by
  27|                         $25,000 or .1%. The District was audited in October 2016 as part of the yearly financial audit by the account-
  28|                         ing firm of Melanson, Heath and Co. from Greenfield, MA and a very good report is anticipated.
  29|                                                                                       Grants and Contracts
  30|                          Monty Tech continues to pursue grant funding on an annual basis. These funds help provide many education-
  31|                         al and social services to the student population. For fiscal year 2016, state and federal grant sources provided
  32|                         the school with $918,426. Programs funded by these grants include: Essential Health Services, Social Inter-
  33|                         vention and Mediation, Improving Teacher Quality, Special Education Services and Program Improvement,
  34|                         Title I Support, Perkins Occupational Education, and Marine Corp Junior Reserve Officer Training. The Dis-
  35|                         trict also received a competitive grant for $136,412 to purchase equipment for the anticipated new Veterinary
  36|                         Science Program. Using these allocation and competitive funds, the school was able to purchase a variety of
  37|                         instructional technology, equipment, and supplies to enhance the learning experience across both academic
  38|                          and vocational programs.
  39|                                                                                     Academic Achievement
  40|                         In 2015-2016, students at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School continued to demonstrate high
  41|                          academic achievement, earning commendable MCAS scores in English Language Arts, Mathematics and Bi-
  42|                         ology. For spring 2016, Monty Tech's passing rate on the English Language Arts was 100%, Mathematics
  43|                         99%, Biology 99%.

  14|                           The District continues to make progress toward narrowing proficiency gaps. Students in all subgroups have
  15|                           met their progress and performance targets, securing the school's Level 1 Status distinction.
  16|                           Vocational Projects in the District Communities
  17|                           Unlike students in traditional, comprehensive high schools, students at Monty Tech are asked to put their
  18|                           education into practice on a daily basis. Students across the twenty different vocational-technical education
  19|                           programs are building homes, reconstructing damaged properties, repairing service vehicles, making walk-
  20|                           ways more accessible, and performing countless community services. The 2015-2016 school year was a
  21|                           busy one for our vocational educators, as each trade aimed to provide practical, hands-on learning experi-
  22|                            ences for our students, while helping to improve each of the eighteen member communities.
  23|                           Auto Body Collision Repair Technology: The Monty Tech Auto Body program is led by a strong instruc-
  24|                           tional team, always eager to enhance the program to benefit the students. This year instructor Michael
  25|                           Forhan revised the freshman curriculum to expand the detailing component, which has been met with great
  26|                           enthusiasm from students and clients alike. Freshmen and sophomores continue to earn industry recog-
  27|                           nized credentials, including the I-CAR Pro level one certification in refinishing and non-structural damage.
  28|                            This certification not only makes our program graduates more employable, it enables students to maintain
  29|                           the I-CAR gold class certification. Throughout the year, students detailed, repaired, and repainted vehicles,
  30|                           fulfilling more than seventy requests that included: an SUV police vehicle car for the Town of Winchen-
  31|                           don, a wooden statue of Johnny Appleseed, and the antique playground fire truck for the Westminster Lions
  32|                           Club.
  33|                                                                                                    125

===PAGE 126=== (ocr, 49 lines)
   1|                         All freshmen achieved 10-Hour CareerSafe online certification for General Safety, two juniors earned co-
   2|                        operative education placements, demonstrating their technical skills in the workplace, and all senior students
   3|                        received an Environmental Protection Agency certificate. (Total enrollment: 62; 35 males, 27 females)
   4|                        Automotive Technology: As in past years, the Automotive Technology program continues to service a variety
   5|                        of vehicles for faculty, staff, municipalities, and district residents. 2015-2016 proved to be a very busy year for
   6|                        the program, as more than four hundred vehicles were serviced by talented students and instructors. In addition
   7|                        to maintaining the school's fleet of vehicles throughout the year, students were asked to repair a Salvation Army
   8|                        disaster relief vehicle, and it was a pleasure to give back to this deserving organization. Students performed
   9|                        very well in the SkillsUSA state competitions, placing third, fourth, and fifth, with hopes to improve in the com-
  10|                        ing year. A total of five students participated in the Co-Op Program, applying their technical skills in real work
  11|                        settings. Finally, instructors are proud to announce three talented program graduates are working in shops in the
  12|                        area, demonstrating the skills they acquired at Monty Tech. (Total enrollment: 62; 50 males, 12 females)

   1|                            A new curriculum was implemented, bringing lessons in pipe welding to senior students and CAD design

   6|                            Student Support Services
   7|                            During the 2015-2016 school year, Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District provided
   8|                            special services to approximately three hundred and fifteen students — measuring progress of over two hun-
   9|                            dred students on Individual Education Plans (IEPs) and just under one hundred students adhering to individ-
  10|                            ualized Section 504 plans. While the Student Support Services (SSS) Department encompasses special edu-
  11|                            cation, the department provides support and is available to all Monty Tech students.
  12|                            The department includes a full-time nursing staff that administers medications, performs state-mandated
  13|                            health screening exams, and provides, when necessary, health information to the special education team for
  14|                            a student's IEP meeting. The department benefits from a full-time school social worker who participates in
  15|                            departmental meetings and assists students who have needs concerning finances, family issues, homeless-
  16|                            ness, maternity, health issues, and proper food and clothing. The school is also fortunate to have on staff a
  17|                            full-time psychologist, whose role it is to evaluate all students referred for an initial evaluation or who re-
  18|                            quire a three-year re-evaluation. In addition, we have a full-time speech language pathologist, who is availa-
  19|                            ble to assist students with disabilities, assess these students and consult with teachers. Our students also
  20|                            have access to the services of a full-time adjustment counselor and part-time school psychologist. All of
  21|                            these individuals are available for scheduled counseling sessions and mental health emergency treatment, as
  22|                            well as crisis intervention.
  23|                            The school's Director of Student Support Services oversees the District's Special Education Program, which
  24|                           is reviewed annually in May, in accordance with regulatory requirements. The comprehensive review and
  25|                            evaluation are done in collaboration with the Parent Advisory Council, and the results of the evaluation are
  26|                            used to improve the special education procedures and programs in place at Montachusett Regional Vocation-
  27|                            al Technical School.

  33|                            maintain constant temperature and humidity supporting the growing demands of the MDF.
  34|                            Three Chromebook mobile labs were added to the fleet of mobile computer labs that circulate among class-
  35|                            rooms providing the students with modern technology. A self-service LobbyGuard kiosk system was imple-
  36|                            mented that manages visitors to the building and increases school safety measures. The cafeteria received an
  37|                            upgrade to their POS system, utilizing advanced technology in school nutrition and meal planning. Candi-
  38|                            dates for free and reduced lunch may now complete an application online, managing payments and fees us-
  39|                           ing this new online system. Finally, in preparation of the anticipated 2016-2017 Chromebook roll-out, the
  40|                            technology staff was reorganized. Duties were reallocated among staff and leadership, transitioning to a
  41|                           more efficient department. New systems and policies are continually being researched to support this initia-

   1|                                                                                              SkillsUSA
   2|                         SkillsUSA is a partnership of students, teachers and industry working together to ensure America has a skilled
   3|                         workforce. Through our association with SkillsUSA, our students develop job skills, leadership and profes-
   4|                         sional skills, as well as provide community service. The 2015-2016 school year was an extraordinary one for
   5|                        our chapter of SkillsUSA. The students met the challenges of districts, performed well at states and prepared
   6|                         for the National Conference. They also conducted several community service projects and raised money for
   7|                        various deserving charities. Serving as co-advisors were Kelsey Moskiwitz, English Instructor, Anne Marie
   8|                         Cataldo, Early Childhood Education Instructor, and Brad Pelletier, Special Education Instructor. Highlights of
   9|                        the year include:
  10|                        In September 2015, forty-one students applied and were accepted to form the Monty Tech SkillsUSA Leader-
  11|                               ship Team. After two leadership training sessions, seven officers were elected.
  12|                         In November 2015, sixteen students, consisting of chapter officers and Leadership Team members attended the
  13|                              Annual Fall State Leadership Conference, where they participated in workshops and leadership exercises
  14|                              and performed community service at an area YMCA Day Camp.
  15|                         The SkillsUSA local competitions took place November 2015, and on December 18'h, the students learned who
  16|                               would advance to the next round of competition.
  17|                        A total of forty-seven medals were captured at the District Competition held in March 2016 at Bay Path Re-
  18|                               gional Vocational Technical School: fourteen gold, sixteen silver and seventeen bronze medals. Outstand-
  19|                              ing student Grace Kirrane qualified to run for the State Executive Council, as well.
  20|                        Olivia Houle, a junior in the Welding program, was selected to serve on the State Advisory Committee to help
  21|                               aid in the planning of the State Conference.
  22|                         Thirty-four district medalists and qualifiers, nine local leadership and occupational related event contestants,
  23|                              one state officer candidate and nineteen voting delegates for a total of sixty-three students attended the
  24|                              State Leadership and Skills Conference, held in April 2016 at Blackstone Valley Vocational Technical
  25|                              School. There, seven students were awarded gold medals, and earned the right to compete at the very com-
  26|                              petitive National Leadership & Skills Conference held in late June.
  27|                         Nine students and six instructors attended the National Leadership & Skills Conference in Louisville, KY in
  28|                              June 2016. There, Taylor Sadowski, a graduate from the Health Occupations program, earned a silver
  29|                              medal in the Medical Assisting contest.
  30|                        In August 2016, Grace Kirrane attended the SkillsUSA Massachusetts state leadership training where she was
  31|                              elected to serve as the SkillsUSA Massachusetts State Vice President.

  15|                          by the Commanding General Marine Corps Training & Education Command. Only ten percent of Marine
  16|                          Corps JROTC programs nationwide receive this recognition. The selection was based on several criteria,
  17|                          including cadet citizenship, the percentage of cadets involved in student government organizations, the num-
  18|                          ber of cadets active in school activities and clubs, the number of community service hours for the unit as a
  19|                          whole and per cadet, the number of organizations assisted by the unit, and scholastic achievements including
  20|                          the number of academic awards and scholarships given to cadets. The units were also measured by participa-
  21|                          tion in public affairs events, drill team competitions, rifle team competitions, physical fitness team competi-
  22|                          tions, and field trips.
  23|                          Because of this prestigious status, Monty Tech's Senior Marine Instructor has the authority to make nomi-
  24|                          nation recommendations to the U.S. Naval Academy, U.S. Air Force Academy, and the Military Academy at
  25|                          West Point under the Honor School Category.
  26|                          Women in Technology
  27|                          Monty Tech is one of the original members of the North Central Massachusetts Women In Technology Pro-
  28|                          gram, and the 2015-2016 school year marked the school's 14 year in this innovative school-to-business
  29|                          partnership. The highly successful collaborative program continues to grow with membership comprised of
  30|                          students from six area high schools. Its mission is to encourage female students to explore well-paying ca-
  31|                          reers in the fields of business and technology. Participants spend two days a month, working on real-world
  32|                          work projects under the mentorship of company managers at Simplex Grinnell and Tyco Safety Products in
  33|                          Westminster, subsidiaries of Tyco International, a Fortune 500 company.
  34|                          The program has been so successful in its mission of providing a proven pathway to corporate America, that
  35|                          it was recently recognized as one of the region's premier experience-based educational programs of its kind.
  36|                          Affiliation with the program, and skills acquired through participation, open doors to career opportunities not
  37|                          otherwise available to high school students. Graduates of the program are also equipped with a foundation to
  38|                          better meet the challenges of an ever-changing and demanding work force.
  39|                           Each year, graduates of the program go on to rewarding and well-paying careers, made possible by this
  40|                          unique experience.
  41|                                                                                                    133

   1|                        Student Athletics
   2|                         The Monty Tech athletic program continues to expand in scope and skill each year. This past year we set a
   3|                        new high for the number of student/athlete participants, with forty-five teams and more than six hundred par-
   4|                        ticipants. Seventeen teams competed in fall 2015, fourteen during the winter season, and another fifteen teams
   5|                        represented the school in spring 2016.
   6|                         Last fall, the Varsity Football team had their best season ever as they went 10 - 2 and won the State Vocation-
   7|                        al Large School Championship. The JV Football team had a good overall season and competed every game.
   8|                         The Freshmen Football team was 1 - 7 - 1. The Varsity Boys Soccer team finished at 14 - 6 and qualified for
   9|                        the post-season tournament, finishing second in the Colonial Athletic League with an 8 - 3 record. The JV
  10|                         Boys Soccer team finished their season with a record of 9 - 6. The Varsity Golf team was 4 - 12, while the JV
  11|                        Golf team played in three tournaments, gaining valuable experience on the links. The Varsity Field Hockey
  12|                        team was 17 - 1 - 2, qualifying for the Central Mass Tournament for the sixth consecutive year. They beat
  13|                        Grafton, 3 - 0 but lost to Lunenburg 1- 0 in an excellently played game. They won the Colonial Athletic
  14|                         League Championship with a 10 - 0 - 2 record. The JV Field Hockey team finished their season with a record
  15|                        of 5 - 2 - 1. The Boys Cross Country team was 9 - 4 and finished 4th in the CAL. The Girls Cross Country
  16|                        team was 3 - 5 overall and also finished 4' in the Colonial Athletic League. The Varsity Girls Volleyball
  17|                        team went 7 - 13, and during their busy season held a successful Bump-Set-Spike competition, raising more
  18|                        than $3,000 to fight Breast Cancer. The JV Girls Volleyball team was 11 - 9, while the Freshmen Girls team
  19|                        continued to improve with a 7 - 10 record. The Varsity Girls Soccer team was 11 - 7 - 1 and qualified for the
  20|                        post-season, where they lost to Bromfield 5 - O. The JV Girls Soccer team finished at 7 - 4 - 2 and will send
  21|                        some fine players to the varsity next year.
  22|                        The Girls Varsity Basketball finished at 14 - 7 on the season, qualifying for the post-season tournament where
  23|                        they lost to an impressive Millis team. The JV Girls were 18 - 0 and will send some fine players up to the var-
  24|                        sity next year, and the Freshmen Girls finished the season with a 9 - 5 record. The Varsity Boys Basketball
  25|                        team finished at 15 - 8 and qualified for both the State Vocational Tournament and the Districts. They lost to
  26|                        Worcester Tech in the first round of the Vocational Tournament and beat Bethany Christian in the first game
  27|                        of the Districts before losing to Hopedale in the quarterfinals. The JV Boys Basketball team was 13 - 8, while
  28|                        the Freshmen Boys ended the season 4 - 12. The Boys Ice Hockey team finished at 9 - 8 - 3 and qualified for
  29|                        the District Tournament, and can expect some impressive JV team members to move up and strengthen the
  30|                        program next season. Both Boys and Girls Indoor Track & Field participated in the Dual Valley Conference
  31|                        in 2016, where the boys were 4 - 4 and the girls finished with a 1 - 7 record.
  32|                        In the spring, the Varsity Softball team qualified for the Central Mass Tournament for the 23°d consecutive
  33|                        year with a 13 - 7 record. They advanced to the Semi-Finals, but fell to Uxbridge. Coach Reid won his 600th
  34|                        game during this season, his 43rd year at Monty Tech. Coach Reid was also selected as the Massachusetts
  35|                        Softball Coach of the Year. The JV Softball team ended their season 5 - 10. The Varsity Boys Volleyball
  36|                        team was 19 - 4 and 12 - 0 in the Colonial Athletic League, winning the league Championship and qualifying
  37|                        for both the State Vocational and District Tournaments. They advanced to the State Vocational Tournament
  38|                        Finals, but fell to Greater New Bedford Regional Vocational technical High School 3 - O. The Varsity Base-
  39|                        ball team finished at 11 - 9, winning the Colonial Athletic League Small School Championship. The JV Base-
  40|                        ball team was 8 - 8 and the Freshmen Baseball team was 2 - 8. The Boys Track & Field team was 8 - 2, fin-
  41|                        ishing 3rd in the Colonial Athletic League, while the Girls Track & Field team ended the season with a record
  42|                        of 8 - 4, placing 5th in the Colonial Athletic League. The Varsity Boys Lacrosse team played in nineteen
  43|                        games, finishing the season with a 9 - 10 record, missing the playoffs by only one game, and finishing 2nd in
  44|                        the Colonial Athletic League. The JV Boys Lacrosse was 11 - 6 - 1, as we look to the future.

  46|                        ister. Dave Reid, Monty Tech's long-time Athletic Director, also received the prestigious "John Young
  47|                        Award" from MIAA for his outstanding contribution and service to high school athletics throughout his forty-
  48|                        three years of service.
